    f_{LMN} &\equiv -\frac{\sqrt \pi}{12}
    % \sum_{L,M,N}^{\text{ord}} C_LC_MC_NC_{LMN}(\pi x)^{\Delta_L + \Delta_M+\Delta_N}
    % \nn\\& 
    \frac{\Gamma(\Delta_L + 1)\Gamma(\Delta_M + 1)\Gamma(\Delta_N + 1)}{\Gamma\lb 1+ \frac{\Delta_L + \Delta_M-\Delta_N}{2}\rb\Gamma\lb 1+ \frac{\Delta_M + \Delta_N-\Delta_L}{2}\rb\Gamma\lb 1+ \frac{\Delta_N + \Delta_L-\Delta_M}{2}\rb\Gamma\lb  \frac{\Delta_L + \Delta_M+\Delta_N + 3}{2}\rb}
\end{align}
Consider the simple case, $\Delta_L = \Delta_M = \Delta_N $, then we obtain, 
\begin{align}
    \p_n\mathcal{T}_n(\Delta_L,\Delta_L,\Delta_L)|_{n=1} &= -\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{12}C_L^3C_{LLL}(\pi x)^{3\Delta} \frac{\Gamma(\Delta_L+1)^3}{\Gamma(1+\Delta_L/2)^3\Gamma((3+3\Delta_L)/2)} \\
    &\equiv C_L^3 C_{LLL} f_{\Delta_L,\Delta_M,\Delta_N} (\pi x)^{3\Delta_L}\\
    &= -C_L^3 C_{LLL}\frac{2^{3\Delta_L}\Gamma \lb\frac{1+\Delta_L}{2}\rb^3}{12\pi \Gamma\lb\frac{3+3\Delta_L}{2}\rb}(\pi x)^{3\Delta_L} 
\end{align}
which agrees with the result of \cite{S_rosi_2018}.

If we choose one of the operators to be vacuum, the above result won't reproduce the result of two-point function we had earlier. This is because, to start with we assumed that the operators $\mO_L, \mO_M,\mO_N$ are non-vacuum operators. We can then use the result of two-point function in \cite{S_rosi_2018}, which matches with the replica trick result from \cite{calabrese2011entanglement}, and denote, 
\begin{align}
    f_{LL} &\equiv \frac{\sqrt{\pi }\Gamma(\Delta_L+1)}{4 \Gamma(\Delta_L + 3/2)}.
\end{align}
Note that, if we substitute $\mO_N$ as the vacuum operator $(I \equiv \mO_0)$, then
\begin{align}
    f_{LL0} &=-\frac{\sqrt \pi}{12}\frac{\Gamma(\Delta_L+1)}{\Gamma(\Delta_L+3/2)} \\
    &=-f_{LL}/3.
\end{align}


\section{\texorpdfstring{Analytic continuation of three-point function $\mu_G^{\Psi|\Phi}$}{Analytic continuation of three-point function mu large interval}}\label{Appendix: analytic continuation of muG three point mixture}
In this section, we follow the method of modular flow used in \cite{S_rosi_2018} to analytically continue the contribution of three-point function $\braket{\mO_{L'}\mO_{L'}\mO_L}$ to the relative entropy in $x\to 1^-$ limit of a pure state with respect to binary mixture. Let the dimension of $\mO_{L'}$ be $\Delta_{L'}$ and the dimension of $\mO_L$ be $\Delta_L$. When we compute $S(\rho_\Psi \| \lb y\rho_\Psi + (1-y)\rho_\Phi\rb)$, the three-point function that contributes to it from the denominator of $\mathcal{F}_n$ takes the following form,
\begin{align}\label{eq: definition of muG q and Gp(n)}
    \mu_G^{\Psi|\Phi} (n,x,y;\Delta_{L'},\Delta_L) \equiv \mu_G &= \sum_{p=1}^{n-1}   \frac{q^p (n-p)D_n(\ep)^{p}}{\lb \sin(\pi p/n)\rb^{2\Delta_{L'}-\Delta_L}} \lb C_\Phi^L G_p(n) + C_\Psi^L G_{n-p}(n)\rb \\
    q &= y^{-1}-1 \\
    % \lambda_p &= D_n(\ep)^p \lb \frac{\sin(\pi (1-x)/n)}{\sin (\pi p /n)}\rb^{2\Delta_{L'}}\\
    G_p(n) &= \sum_{m=1}^{p-1} \frac{1}{\lb \sin(\pi m/n) \sin(\pi (p-m)/n)\rb^{\Delta_L}}
\end{align}
We want to compute the analytic continuation of the derivative of $\mu_G$ as $n\to 1$. Let us define,
\begin{align}
    \lambda_n(\ep) \equiv  qD_n(\ep).
\end{align}
It is clear that, at $n=1$, 
\begin{align}
    D_1(\ep) = 1, \qquad\qquad \lambda_1 = q = \frac{1-y}{y}.
\end{align}
We now define the weighted three-point replica sum in terms of the new variables (call $\lambda \equiv \lambda_n(\ep))$,
\begin{align}
    A_n(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},\Delta_L) &\equiv \sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\lambda^p \frac{G_p(n)}{\lb\sin \frac{\pi p}{n}\rb^{2\Delta_{L'}-\Delta_L}}\\
    &= \sum_{p=1}^{n-1}\sum_{m=1}^{p-1}\frac{\lambda^p}{\lb\sin \frac{\pi m}{n}\rb^{\Delta_L} \lb\sin \frac{\pi (p-m)}{n}\rb^{\Delta_L} \lb\sin \frac{\pi p}{n}\rb^{2\Delta_{L'}-\Delta_L}}
\end{align}
Then, in order to write $\mu_G$ in terms of $A_n(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},\Delta_L)$, the first term is straight forward,
\begin{align}
    \sum_{p=1}^{n-1} \frac{q^p D_n(\ep)^p(n-p)G_p(n)}{\lb\sin \frac{\pi p}{n}\rb^{2\Delta_{L'}-\Delta_L}} =\lb n-\lambda \p_\lambda\rb A_n(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},\Delta_L).
\end{align}
For the second term, make the change $r = n-p$. Then we obtain,
\begin{align}
    \sum_{p=1}^{n-1} \frac{q^p D_n(\ep)^p(n-p)G_{n-p}(n)}{\lb\sin \frac{\pi p}{n}\rb^{2\Delta_{L'}-\Delta_L}} &= \lambda^n\sum_{p=1}^{n-1} r\lambda^{-r} \frac{G_r(n)}{\lb\sin \frac{\pi p}{n}\rb^{2\Delta_{L'}-\Delta_L}}
\end{align}
Let $z = \lambda^{-1}$, then we finally have,
\begin{align}
    \mu_G &= C_\Phi^L\lb n-\lambda \p_\lambda\rb A_n(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},\Delta_L) + C_\Psi^L \lambda^n \lb z\p_z A_n(z)\rb|_{z = \lambda^{-1}} \\
    \implies \p_n \mu_G |_{n=1}&= C_\Phi^L(1-\lambda \p_\lambda)\p_nA_n(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},\Delta_L)|_{n=1} + C_\Psi^L \lambda [z\p_z \lb  \p_nA_n(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},\Delta_L)|_{n=1}\rb]_{z=\lambda^{-1}}
\end{align}
From our previous result of the analytic continuation of the weighted two-point function in Appendix \ref{analytic continuation of theta or mu(n)}, and that of the three-point function \ref{Analytic continuation of T_n,LMN}, we can analogously define, 
\begin{align}
    \nu &\equiv \frac{\log \lambda}{2\pi} \\
    \implies  \lambda^{\frac{1}{2} - \frac{is_2}{2\pi}} &= \sqrt \lambda e^{-i\nu s_2} .
\end{align}
Then the integral we need to compute is the following,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{I}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) &= \sqrt \lambda \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}ds_1 \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} ds_2 \, e^{-i\nu s_2} \frac{i(s_2-s_1)}{2\pi} \nn\\
    &\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\frac{i}{\lb\cosh\frac{s_1}{2}\rb^{\Delta_L+1}\lb\cosh\frac{s_2}{2}\rb^{2\Delta_{L'}-\Delta_L+1}\lb\sinh\frac{s_2-s_1}{2}\rb\Big[\lb i\sinh\frac{s_2 - s_1}{2}\rb^2\Big]^{\Delta_L/2}}
\end{align}
Also, we have the following relation,
\begin{align}\label{eq: relation of A_n and I_lamnbda}
    \p_nA_n(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},\Delta_L)|_{n=1}&=\frac{3}{16\pi}\mathcal{I}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}).
\end{align}
Similarly, we can introduce the auxiliary weighted integral,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) &= \sqrt \lambda \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}ds_1 \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} ds_2 \,  
    % \nn\\ &\qquad\qquad\qquad\times
    \frac{ie^{-i\nu s_2} }{\lb\cosh\frac{s_1}{2}\rb^{\Delta_L+1}\lb\cosh\frac{s_2}{2}\rb^{2\Delta_{L'}-\Delta_L+1}\lb\sinh\frac{s_2-s_1}{2}\rb\Big[\lb i\sinh\frac{s_2 - s_1}{2}\rb^2\Big]^{\Delta_L/2}}
\end{align}
Note that, for $\lambda = 1$, this reduces to $\mathcal{J}(\Delta_L,2\Delta_{L'}-\Delta_L,\Delta_L)$ specialization of our auxiliary integral defined in the previous Appendix \ref{Analytic continuation of T_n,LMN}. Now we can Fourier decouple $u = s_2 - s_1$. Define,
\begin{align}
    \hat G_\Delta(\omega) &= \int_{-\infty}^\infty ds \frac{e^{iqs}}{\lb\cosh\frac{s}{2}\rb^{\Delta +1}}=\frac{2^{1+\Delta}\Gamma\lb\frac{1+\Delta}{2}+ iq\rb\Gamma\lb\frac{1+\Delta}{2}-iq\rb}{\Gamma\lb\Delta +1\rb}.
\end{align}
After Fourier decoupling of $u = s_2 - s_1$ and deformation of contour $u \to u + i\pi$, we obtain,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) = \sqrt \lambda \int_{-\infty}^\infty \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-\pi \omega} \hat G_{\Delta_L}(\omega)^2 \hat G_{2\Delta_{L'}-\Delta_L}(\omega+\nu)
\end{align}
Note that, this is the generalization of the previous three-point unweighted integral. We can then directly write down the actual integral,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{I}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) &= \sqrt \lambda \int_{-\infty}^\infty \frac{d\omega}{2\pi}  \hat G_{\Delta_L}(\omega) \hat G_{2\Delta_{L'}-\Delta_L}(\omega+\nu) \frac{1}{2\pi}\p_\omega \Big[e^{-\pi \omega}\hat G_{\Delta_L}(\omega)\Big]
\end{align}
We want to write the above integral in terms of $\mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'})$. We define,
\begin{align}
    A &\equiv \sqrt \lambda \int_{-\infty}^\infty \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-\pi \omega} \hat G_{\Delta_L}(\omega)\hat G'_{\Delta_L}(\omega) \hat G_{2\Delta_{L'}-\Delta_L}(\omega+\nu) \\
    K &\equiv \sqrt \lambda \int_{-\infty}^\infty \frac{d\omega}{2\pi} e^{-\pi \omega} \hat G_{\Delta_L}(\omega)^2\hat G'_{2\Delta_{L'}-\Delta_L}(\omega+\nu).
\end{align}
Then integrating the total derivative, we obtain,
\begin{align}
    -\pi \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) + 2A + K = 0 
\end{align}
Since we defined, $\lambda = e^{2\pi \nu}$, we then have,
\begin{align}
    \p_\nu \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) = \pi \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) + K.
\end{align}
Eliminating $K$, we get,
\begin{align}
    &-\pi \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) + 2A + \p_\nu \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) - \pi \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) = 0,
    \\
    \implies &A = \pi \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) - \frac{1}{2}\p_\nu \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}).
\end{align}
But we have,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{I}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) &= \frac{1}{2\pi} \lb A - \pi \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'})\rb.
\end{align}
Therefore,
\begin{align}\label{eq: relation between I and J lambda}
    \mathcal{I}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) &= -\frac{1}{4\pi} \p_\nu \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'})\\
    &= -\frac{1}{2}\lambda \p_\lambda \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) .
\end{align}
% Note that, for $\lambda = 1$, if we set $\Delta_{L'} = \Delta_L$, we obtain 
% \begin{align}
%     \mathcal{I}_1(\Delta_L,\Delta_L)&= -\frac{1}{3}\mathcal{J}_1(\Delta_L,\Delta_L),
% \end{align}
% that matches with the results of \cite{S_rosi_2018}. 
Now we consider the case, when $\Delta_L\in 2\mathbb Z_{\geq 0}$. This gets rid of branch cuts. By exchanging the integration variables as done in previous appendix, we thus end up with the following integral,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) &= i^{\Delta_L+1} 2^{3-\Delta_L} \sqrt \lambda \int_{-1/2}^{1/2} dv_1 \int_{-1/2}^{1/2}dv_2 \frac{(1-4v_1^2)^{\Delta_L}(1-4v_2^2)^{\Delta_{L'}}}{(v_2 - v_1 + i0^+)^{\Delta_L+1}}\lb \frac{1+2v_2}{1-2v_2}\rb^{-i\nu}.
\end{align}
Here the extra factor arises from the phase, i.e.
\begin{align}
    e^{-i\nu s_2} = \lb\frac{1+2v_2}{1-2v_2}\rb^{-i\nu}.
\end{align}
We map the integration region to $[0,1]$ by the following change of variables,
\begin{align}
    x = v_1 + 1/2, \qquad t = v_2 + 1/2.
\end{align}
Then we have,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) &= i^{\Delta_L+1} 2^{3+ \Delta_L+ 2\Delta_{L'}} \sqrt \lambda \int_0^1 dx \int_0^1 dt \frac{x^{\Delta_L} (1-x)^{\Delta_L}t^{\Delta_{L'}- i\nu}(1-t)^{\Delta_{L'}+i\nu}}{(t-x+ i0^+)^{\Delta_L + 1}}.
\end{align}
For integer $d$ we have the following integral,
\begin{align}
    Q_d(z) &= \frac{1}{2^{d+1}}\int_{-1}^1du,\frac{(1-u^2)^d}{(z-u)^{d+1}}.
\end{align}
Set $z = 2t- 1+i0^+$, and $u = 2x-1$. Then we have, 
\begin{align}
    Q_d(2t-1+i0^+) &= \frac{1}{2^{d+1}} \int_0^12dx\, \frac{\lb  4x(1-x)\rb^d}{\lb 2(t-x)) + i0^+\rb^{d}} \\
    &= \frac{1}{2}\int_0^1dx\frac{x^d(1-x)^d}{(t-x+i0^+)^{d+1}}.
\end{align}
Using this, we obtain,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) &= i^{\Delta_L+1} 2^{3+ \Delta_L+ 2\Delta_{L'}}\sqrt \lambda \int_0^1 dt\,
    t^{\Delta_{L'}-i\nu}(1-t)^{\Delta_{L'}+i\nu}2Q_{\Delta_L}(2t-1+i0^+).
\end{align}
We also have the following relation between the Legendre function of the first and second kind,
\begin{align}
    Q_d(x+ i0^+) &= Q_d(x) - \frac{i \pi }{2}P_d(x) \\
    \frac{2}{\pi} \sin(\pi d) Q_d(x) &= \cos(\pi d) P_d(x) - P_d(-x).
\end{align}
Therefore,
\begin{align}
    2Q_d(x+i0^+) &= \frac{\pi }{\sin (\pi d)} \big[e^{-i\pi d} P_d(x) - P_d(-x) \big]
\end{align}
Substituting $x = 2t-1$, we get,
\begin{align}
    2Q_d(2t- 1+i0^+) &= \frac{\pi }{\sin (\pi d)} \big[e^{-i\pi d} P_d(2t-1) - P_d(1-2t) \big]
\end{align}
For the simplicity of notation, lets define,
\begin{align}
    a = \Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu, \qquad b = \Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu, \qquad \mathcal{C}_{\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}} = i^{\Delta_L+1}2^{3+ \Delta_L+ 2\Delta_{L'}}.
\end{align}
Thus we end up with,
\begin{align}\label{eq: Legendre representation of J_lambda}
    \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) &= \mathcal{C}_{\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}} \sqrt \lambda \frac{\pi}{\sin (\pi \Delta_L)} \Big[ e^{-i\pi \Delta_L}\int_0^1 dt\, t^{a-1}(1-t)^{b-1} P_{\Delta_L}(2t-1) \nn \\
    &\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad- \int_0^1 dt \, t^{a-1}(1-t)^{b-1}P_{\Delta_L}(1-2t)\Big]
\end{align}
For any general $\Delta_L$, we have the hypergeometric representation of the Legendre function,
\begin{align}
    P_d(x) &={} _2F_1\lb -d,d+1;1;\frac{1-x}{2}\rb
\end{align}
and the identity,
\begin{align}
    \int_0^1 dt\, t^{A-1}(1-t)^{B-1} \,_2F_1\lb \alpha ,\beta;\gamma;t\rb &= B(a,b) \,{}_3F_2\!\left(
\begin{matrix}
\alpha,\beta,A\\
\gamma,A+B
\end{matrix}
;1
\right)
\end{align}
Using the hypergeometric representation and the identity above, we get,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) &= i^{\Delta_L+1}2^{3+\Delta_L+2\Delta_{L'}}\sqrt \lambda \frac{\pi B(\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu,\Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu)}{\sin(\pi \Delta_L)} \nn\\
    &\times \Bigg[ e^{-i\pi \Delta_L} {}_3F_2\lb \begin{matrix}
        -\Delta_L , \Delta_L+1,\Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu \\1,2\Delta_{L'}+2
    \end{matrix};1\rb - {}_3F_2\lb \begin{matrix}
        -\Delta_L , \Delta_L+1,\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu \\1,2\Delta_{L'}+2
    \end{matrix};1\rb\Bigg]
\end{align}
Define,
\begin{align}\label{eq: H_DeltaLDeltaL' (nu)}
    \mathcal{H}_{\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}}(\nu) &=  \frac{\pi B(a,b)}{\sin(\pi\Delta_L)}\Bigg[ e^{-i\pi \Delta_L} {}_3F_2\lb \begin{matrix}
        -\Delta_L , \Delta_L+1,b \\1,2\Delta_{L'}+2
    \end{matrix};1\rb - {}_3F_2\lb \begin{matrix}
        -\Delta_L , \Delta_L+1,a \\1,2\Delta_{L'}+2
    \end{matrix};1\rb\Bigg]
\end{align}
Then we have,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) &= i^{\Delta_L+1}2^{3+\Delta_L+2\Delta_{L'}}e^{\pi \nu}\mathcal{H}_{\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}}(\nu),
    \\
    \implies \mathcal{I}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'})&= -\frac{i^{\Delta_L+1}2^{3+\Delta_L+2\Delta_{L'}}e^{\pi \nu}}{4\pi}\lb \pi \mathcal{H}_{\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}}(\nu)+ \mathcal{H}'_{\Delta_l,\Delta_{L'}}(\nu)\rb
\end{align}
We can now compute the derivative of $\mu_G$ with respect to $n$ as $n\to 1$. We have,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{I}&\equiv \mathcal{I}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}) ,\qquad \mathcal{H}\equiv \mathcal{H}_{\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}}(\nu)
    \\
    \implies (1-\lambda\p_\lambda)\mathcal{I} &= \frac{C_{\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}}e^{\pi\nu}}{8\pi^2}\big[ -2\pi \lb \pi \mathcal{H} + \mathcal{H}'\rb +\pi^2\mathcal{H} + 2\pi \mathcal{H}' + \mathcal{H}'' \big]
\end{align}
and
\begin{align}
    \lambda(z\p_z \mathcal{I})|_{z=\lambda^{-1}} &= -\frac{C_{\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}}e^{\pi\nu}}{8} \big[ \mathcal{H}(-\nu) + \frac{2}{\pi}\mathcal{H}'(-\nu) + \frac{1}{\pi^2}\mathcal{H}''(-\nu)\big]
\end{align}
Using the above two, we obtain,
\begin{align}\label{eq: analytic continuation of muG three point large x mixture}
    \p_n\mu_G^{\Psi|\Phi} (n,x,y;\Delta_{L'},\Delta_L)|_{n=1} &= \frac{3i^{\Delta_L+1}2^{3+\Delta_L+2\Delta_{L'}}e^{\pi \nu}}{128\pi} \Bigg[ C_\Phi \lb -\mathcal{H}_{\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}}(\nu) + \frac{\mathcal{H}''_{\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}}(\nu)}{\pi^2} \rb \nn \\
    &\qquad\qquad\qquad- C_\Psi \lb \mathcal{H}_{\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}}(-\nu) + \frac{2}{\pi}\mathcal{H}'_{\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}}(-\nu)+\frac{\mathcal{H}''_{\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'}}(-\nu)}{\pi^2}\rb\Bigg]
\end{align}
\section{Analytic continuation sub-leading term for coherent states}\label{appendix: sub leading term for coherent states}
We want to compute the sub-leading contribution of relative entropy of mixture of coherent states. There are two contributions, as given in (\ref{eq: 3 point function coherent states mixture}). We compute those contributions separately in this appendix. 
\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Analytic continuation of the contribution from $\hat L^{\rm sum}_p$}{Analytic continuation of three point function Lp sum}}
We consider $\Delta_L = \Delta_{i\p\phi} = 1$. Note that the analytic continuation of three point function (\ref{eq: analytic continuation of muG three point large x mixture}) is not defined at $\Delta_L=1$. If we work with $\mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta_L,\Delta_{L'})$, it can be shown that the square bracket in (\ref{eq: Legendre representation of J_lambda}) vanishes for $\Delta_L\in \mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}$. For $\Delta_L\in \mathbb{Z}$, we have,
\begin{align}
    P_{\Delta_L}(-x) &= (-1)^{\Delta_L}P_{\Delta_L}(x).
\end{align}
Thus, the square bracket in (\ref{eq: Legendre representation of J_lambda}) evaluates to,
\begin{align}
    \Big[ e^{-i\pi \Delta_L}&\int_0^1 dt\, t^{a-1}(1-t)^{b-1} P_{\Delta_L}(2t-1) - \int_0^1 dt \, t^{a-1}(1-t)^{b-1}P_{\Delta_L}(1-2t)\Big] 
    \nn\\
    &\propto  \Big[ (-1)^{\Delta_L} \int_0^1 dt\, t^{a-1}(1-t)^{b-1} P_{\Delta_L}(2t-1) \nn\\&\qquad- (-1)^{\Delta_L} \int_0^1 dt \, t^{a-1}(1-t)^{b-1}P_{\Delta_L}(2t-1)\Big] \\
    &=0.
\end{align}
Hence, we need to take limit properly to obtain the analytic continuation at integer $\Delta_L$. For simplicity, denote,
\begin{align}
    F_z(\Delta) &\equiv {}_3F_2\lb \begin{matrix}
        -\Delta , \Delta+1,z \\1,2M
    \end{matrix};1\rb \\
    M&\equiv \Delta_{L'}+1\\
    N(\Delta)&\equiv e^{-i\pi \Delta}F_b(\Delta) - F_a(\Delta).
\end{align}
Then, 
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{J}_\lambda(\Delta,\Delta_{L'}) &= i^{\Delta + 1} 2^{3+\Delta + 2\Delta_{L'}}\sqrt\lambda B(a,b) \frac{\pi N(\Delta)}{\sin(\pi \Delta)}.
\end{align}
We use l'Hopital derivative at $\Delta=1$, 
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{J}_\lambda(1,\Delta_{L'}) &= 2^{4+2\Delta_{L'}}\sqrt\lambda B(a,b)N'(1),
    \\&= 2^{4+2\Delta_{L'}}\sqrt\lambda B(a,b) \lb i\pi F_b(1) - F_b'(1) - F_a'(1) \rb.
\end{align}
We know, 
\begin{align}
    F_b(1) &= -\frac{i \nu}{M}.
\end{align}
We need to evaluate the derivatives of the general Hypergeometric functions. We use the integral representation,
\begin{align}
    P_\Delta(1-2t) &= {}_2F_1(-\Delta,\Delta +1 ;1;t).
\end{align}
Then we have,
\begin{align}
    F_z(\Delta) &= \frac{1}{B(z,2M-z)}\int_0^1 dt\, t^{z-1}(1-t)^{2M-z-1}P_\Delta(1-2t).
\end{align}
Therefore, 
\begin{align}
    F'_z(1) &= \frac{1}{B(z,2M-z)}\int_0^1 dt\, t^{z-1}(1-t)^{2M-z-1}\p_\Delta P_\Delta(1-2t)\Big|_{\Delta = 1}\\
    &= \frac{1}{B(z,2M-z)}\int_0^1 dt\, t^{z-1}(1-t)^{2M-z-1}[(1-2t)\log (1-t) -2t]
\end{align}
where in the second line we plugged in the derivative of $P_\Delta$ at $\Delta = 1$. Now to evaluate the Beta-function integrals, we use the following integral,
\begin{align}
    \frac{1}{B(z,2M-z)}\int_0^1 dt\, t^{z}(1-t)^{2M-z-1}\log (1-t)  &= \frac{z}{2M}[\psi(2M-z) - \psi(2M)],\\
    \frac{1}{B(z,2M-z)}\int_0^1 dt\, t^{z}(1-t)^{2M-z-1} &= \frac{z}{2M}.
\end{align}
Hence, 
\begin{align}
    F_z'(1) &= \psi(2M-z) - \psi(2M) - \frac{z}{M}[\psi(2M-z) - \psi(2M+1)] - \frac{z}{M}.
\end{align}
Therefore, 
\begin{align}
    N'(1) &= 2-\frac{1}{M} + \frac{\pi \nu}{M} + \frac{i\nu}{M}[\psi(a)-\psi(b)].
\end{align}
Finally, we obtain,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{J}_\lambda(1,\Delta_{L'}) &= 2^{4+2\Delta_{L'}}\sqrt\lambda B(\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu,\Delta_{L'}+1 + i\nu) \nn \\
    &\times\Big[ 2 - \frac{1}{\Delta_{L'}+1} +\frac{\pi\nu}{\Delta_{L'}+1} + \frac{i\nu}{\Delta_{L'}+1} \lb \psi(\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu) - \psi(\Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu)\rb\Big]
\end{align}
This gives the regular continuation at $\Delta_L$. Now we can proceed to evaluate the contribution of the three-point function. 
% We can now use this result to evaluate the weighted three-point replica sum required for the current contribution. We will eventually be interested in the equal mixture $y=1/2$. In this case, $q=1$, and at $n=1$ we have
% \begin{align}
%     \lambda = 1,\qquad \nu = \frac{\log \lambda}{2\pi}=0.
% \end{align}
% Before setting $\nu=0$, we take the required derivatives with respect to $\nu$. Using $\sqrt{\lambda}=e^{\pi\nu}$, we can rewrite the previous result as
% \begin{align}
%     \mathcal{J}_\lambda(1,\Delta_{L'}) &= 2^{4+2\Delta_{L'}}e^{\pi\nu}
%     B(\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu,\Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu)
%     \nn\\
%     &\quad\times\Bigg[
%     2-\frac{1}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
%     +\frac{\pi\nu}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
%     +\frac{i\nu}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
%     \Big(
%     \psi(\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu)
%     -\psi(\Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu)
%     \Big)
%     \Bigg].
% \end{align}
We first note that
\begin{align}
    \p_\nu \log B(\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu,\Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu)
    &=
    -i\psi(\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu)
    +i\psi(\Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu),
\end{align}
and therefore,
\begin{align}
    \p_\nu B(\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu,\Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu)\Big|_{\nu=0}
    &=0,
    \\
    \p_\nu^2 B(\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu,\Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu)\Big|_{\nu=0}
    &=
    -2B(\Delta_{L'}+1,\Delta_{L'}+1)
    \psi^{(1)}(\Delta_{L'}+1),
\end{align}
where $\psi^{(1)}$ denotes the trigamma function.
For the remaining factor in the square bracket, we use
\begin{align}
    \psi(\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu)
    -
    \psi(\Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu)
    &=
    -2i\nu\,\psi^{(1)}(\Delta_{L'}+1)
    +O(\nu^3).
\end{align}
Hence,
\begin{align}
    &\Bigg[
    2-\frac{1}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
    +\frac{\pi\nu}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
    +\frac{i\nu}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
    \Big(
    \psi(\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu)
    -\psi(\Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu)
    \Big)
    \Bigg]_{\nu=0}
    \nn\\
    &\hspace{4cm}
    =
    2-\frac{1}{\Delta_{L'}+1},
    \\
    &\p_\nu\Bigg[
    2-\frac{1}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
    +\frac{\pi\nu}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
    +\frac{i\nu}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
    \Big(
    \psi(\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu)
    -\psi(\Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu)
    \Big)
    \Bigg]_{\nu=0}
    \nn\\
    &\hspace{4cm}
    =
    \frac{\pi}{\Delta_{L'}+1},
    \\
    &\p_\nu^2\Bigg[
    2-\frac{1}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
    +\frac{\pi\nu}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
    +\frac{i\nu}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
    \Big(
    \psi(\Delta_{L'}+1-i\nu)
    -\psi(\Delta_{L'}+1+i\nu)
    \Big)
    \Bigg]_{\nu=0}
    \nn\\
    &\hspace{4cm}
    =
    \frac{4}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
    \psi^{(1)}(\Delta_{L'}+1).
\end{align}
Combining these terms, the first derivative of $\mathcal{J}_\lambda$ at $\nu=0$ is
\begin{align}\label{eq: del nu J lambda in terms of Beta function}
    \p_\nu \mathcal{J}_\lambda(1,\Delta_{L'})\Big|_{\nu=0}
    &=
    2^{5+2\Delta_{L'}}\pi
    B(\Delta_{L'}+1,\Delta_{L'}+1),
\end{align}
while the second derivative is
\begin{align}
    \p_\nu^2 \mathcal{J}_\lambda(1,\Delta_{L'})\Big|_{\nu=0}
    &=
    2^{4+2\Delta_{L'}}
    B(\Delta_{L'}+1,\Delta_{L'}+1)
    \Bigg[
    \pi^2\lb
    2+\frac{1}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
    \rb
    \nn\\
    &\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad
    +
    \lb
    -4+\frac{6}{\Delta_{L'}+1}
    \rb
    \psi^{(1)}(\Delta_{L'}+1)
    \Bigg].
\end{align}
From (\ref{eq: relation between I and J lambda}), for the equal mixture, we obtain
\begin{align}
    \lb
    1-\lambda\p_\lambda
    \rb
    \mathcal{I}_\lambda(1,\Delta_{L'})
    \Big|_{\lambda=1}
    &=
    -\frac{1}{4\pi}
    \p_\nu\mathcal{J}_\lambda(1,\Delta_{L'})
    \Big|_{\nu=0}
    +
    \frac{1}{8\pi^2}
    \p_\nu^2\mathcal{J}_\lambda(1,\Delta_{L'})
    \Big|_{\nu=0}
    \\
    &=
    -\frac{
    2^{2\Delta_{L'}+1}
    B(\Delta_{L'}+1,\Delta_{L'}+1)
    }{\Delta_{L'}+1}
    \Bigg[
    2\Delta_{L'}+1
    \nn\\
    &\qquad\qquad\qquad
    +
    \frac{2(2\Delta_{L'}-1)}{\pi^2}
    \psi^{(1)}(\Delta_{L'}+1)
    \Bigg].
\end{align}
Using (\ref{eq: relation of A_n and I_lamnbda}),
we can now apply the above analytic continuation to the
$\hat L_p^{\rm sum}$ contribution in Appendix
\ref{appendix rho alpha wrt averaged binary mixture}.
For $\Delta_L=1$, the function $G_p(n)$ becomes
\begin{align}
    G_p(n)
    &=
    \sum_{r=1}^{p-1}
    \frac{1}{
    \sin(\pi r/n)
    \sin(\pi(p-r)/n)
    }.
\end{align}
Using
\begin{align}
    \cot A+\cot B
    &=
    \frac{\sin(A+B)}{\sin A\sin B},
\end{align}
and relabelling $r\rightarrow p-r$ in the second term, we obtain
\begin{align}\label{eq: Gp(n) identity}
    G_p(n)
    &=
    \frac{2}{\sin(\pi p/n)}
    \sum_{r=1}^{p-1}\cot\lb\frac{\pi r}{n}\rb ,
\end{align}
or equivalently,
\begin{align}
    2\sum_{r=1}^{p-1}\cot\lb\frac{\pi r}{n}\rb
    &=
    \sin\lb\frac{\pi p}{n}\rb G_p(n).
\end{align}
Then,
\begin{align}
    &\delta^2\zeta_n
    \sum_{p=1}^{n-1}(n-p)\lambda^p
    \lb
    \frac{\sin(\pi\ep/n)}
    {\sin(\pi p/n)}
    \rb^{2\Delta_{L'}}
    \sum_{r=1}^{p-1}\cot\lb\frac{\pi r}{n}\rb
    =
    \frac{\delta^2\zeta_n}{2}
    \sin^{2\Delta_{L'}}\lb\frac{\pi\ep}{n}\rb
    \lb n-\lambda\p_\lambda\rb
    A_n(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},1).
\end{align}
For the averaged binary mixture, $\lambda_n(\ep)=D_n(\ep)$ and
$\lambda|_{n=1}=1$. Since $A_1(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},1)=0$ for
arbitrary $\lambda$, the derivatives of $\lambda_n$ and of the
prefactor do not contribute at $n=1$. Hence,
\begin{align}
    \left.
    \Bigg[
    \log 2-
    S\lb
    \rho_\alpha
    \Big\|
    \frac{\rho_\alpha+\rho_\beta}{2}
    \rb
    \Bigg]
    \right|_{\hat L^{\rm sum}}
    &=
    \delta^2\sin^{2\Delta_{L'}+1}(\pi\ep)
    \lb1-\lambda\p_\lambda\rb
    \p_n A_n(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},1)
    \Big|_{\substack{n=1\\\lambda=1}}
    \nn\\
    &=
    -\frac{
    3\delta^2\,2^{2\Delta_{L'}-3}
    B(\Delta_{L'}+1,\Delta_{L'}+1)
    }{
    \pi(\Delta_{L'}+1)
    }
    \nn\\
    &\qquad\times
    \Bigg[
    2\Delta_{L'}+1
    +
    \frac{2(2\Delta_{L'}-1)}{\pi^2}
    \psi^{(1)}(\Delta_{L'}+1)
    \Bigg]
    \sin^{2\Delta_{L'}+1}(\pi\ep).
\end{align}
In the $\ep\to0$ limit, this becomes
\begin{align}
    \left.
    \Bigg[
    \log 2-
    S\lb
    \rho_\alpha
    \Big\|
    \frac{\rho_\alpha+\rho_\beta}{2}
    \rb
    \Bigg]
    \right|_{\hat L^{\rm sum}}
    &=
    -\frac{
    3\delta^2\,2^{2\Delta_{L'}-3}
    B(\Delta_{L'}+1,\Delta_{L'}+1)
    }{
    \pi(\Delta_{L'}+1)
    }
    \nn\\
    &\qquad\times
    \Bigg[
    2\Delta_{L'}+1
    +
    \frac{2(2\Delta_{L'}-1)}{\pi^2}
    \psi^{(1)}(\Delta_{L'}+1)
    \Bigg]
    (\pi\ep)^{2\Delta_{L'}+1}
    +\cdots .
\end{align}
Note that, the above result is symmetric under $\alpha \leftrightarrow \beta$. This was a result particular to coherent states. It need not be true for any general CFT. 
We next consider the contribution from $\hat L_p^{\rm div}$.
\subsection{Analytic continuation of the divergent term contribution}

We now consider the contribution from $\hat L_p^{\rm div}$. Using (\ref{eq: Gp(n) identity}) with
$p\rightarrow n-p$, together with
\begin{align}
    \sum_{r=1}^{n-1}\cot\lb\frac{\pi r}{n}\rb=0,
\end{align}
we obtain
\begin{align}
    G_{n-p}(n)-G_p(n)
    &=
    \frac{2\cot(\pi p/n)}
    {\sin(\pi p/n)}.
\end{align}
The contribution from $\hat L_p^{\rm div}$ is therefore
\begin{align}
    \left.-\log\mathcal{F}_n\right|_{\hat L^{\rm div}}
    &=
    \delta^2\frac{\pi\ep}{n}
    \sin^{2\Delta_{L'}}\lb\frac{\pi\ep}{n}\rb
    \sum_{p=1}^{n-1}
    (n-p)\lambda^p
    \frac{\cot(\pi p/n)}
    {\sin^{2\Delta_{L'}}(\pi p/n)},
\end{align}
where $2\Delta_{L'}=\delta^2$. On the other hand, from the definition of $A_n$,
\begin{align}
    &\lambda^n
    \left.
    z\p_zA_n(z;\Delta_{L'},1)
    \right|_{z=\lambda^{-1}}
    -
    \lb n-\lambda\p_\lambda\rb
    A_n(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},1)
    \nn\\
    &\qquad=
    \sum_{p=1}^{n-1}
    (n-p)\lambda^p
    \frac{G_{n-p}(n)-G_p(n)}
    {\sin^{2\Delta_{L'}-1}(\pi p/n)}
    \nn\\
    &\qquad=
    2\sum_{p=1}^{n-1}
    (n-p)\lambda^p
    \frac{\cot(\pi p/n)}
    {\sin^{2\Delta_{L'}}(\pi p/n)}.
\end{align}
Hence, the divergent contribution can be expressed entirely in terms of the replica sum
$A_n$ as
\begin{align}
    \left.-\log\mathcal{F}_n\right|_{\hat L^{\rm div}}
    &=
    \frac{\delta^2}{2}
    \frac{\pi\ep}{n}
    \sin^{2\Delta_{L'}}\lb\frac{\pi\ep}{n}\rb
    \Bigg[
    \lambda^n
    \left.
    z\p_zA_n(z;\Delta_{L'},1)
    \right|_{z=\lambda^{-1}}
    -
    \lb n-\lambda\p_\lambda\rb
    A_n(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},1)
    \Bigg].
\end{align}
For the averaged binary mixture, $\lambda|_{n=1}=1$. Since
$A_1(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},1)=0$ for arbitrary $\lambda$, we obtain
\begin{align}
    \left.
    \Bigg[
    \log2-
    S\lb
    \rho_\alpha
    \Big\|
    \frac{\rho_\alpha+\rho_\beta}{2}
    \rb
    \Bigg]
    \right|_{\hat L^{\rm div}}
    &=
    \frac{\delta^2}{2}
    (\pi\ep)
    \sin^{2\Delta_{L'}}(\pi\ep)
    \Bigg[
    \lambda\p_\lambda
    -
    \lb1-\lambda\p_\lambda\rb
    \Bigg]
    \p_nA_n(\lambda;\Delta_{L'},1)
    \Big|_{\substack{n=1\\\lambda=1}} .
\end{align}
We will also need $\p_nA_n$ itself at $\lambda=1$. From
(\ref{eq: relation of A_n and I_lamnbda}),
(\ref{eq: relation between I and J lambda}) and (\ref{eq: del nu J lambda in terms of Beta function}), we have
\begin{align}
    \p_nA_n(1;\Delta_{L'},1)\Big|_{n=1}
    &=
    -\frac{3}{64\pi^2}
    \p_\nu\mathcal{J}_\lambda(1,\Delta_{L'})
    \Big|_{\nu=0}
    \nn\\
    &=
    -\frac{
    3\,\lb 2^{2\Delta_{L'}-1}\rb
    }{\pi}
    B(\Delta_{L'}+1,\Delta_{L'}+1).
\end{align}
Combining this with the result for
$\lb1-\lambda\p_\lambda\rb\p_nA_n$ obtained above, we find
\begin{align}
    \left.
    \Bigg[
    \log2-
    S\lb
    \rho_\alpha
    \Big\|
    \frac{\rho_\alpha+\rho_\beta}{2}
    \rb
    \Bigg]
    \right|_{\hat L^{\rm div}}
    &=
    -\frac{
    3\delta^2\,2^{2\Delta_{L'}-3}
    B(\Delta_{L'}+1,\Delta_{L'}+1)
    }{
    \pi(\Delta_{L'}+1)
    }
    \nn\\
    &\quad\times
    \Bigg[
    1-
    \frac{2(2\Delta_{L'}-1)}{\pi^2}
    \psi^{(1)}(\Delta_{L'}+1)
    \Bigg]
    (\pi\ep)^{2\Delta_{L'}+1}
    +\cdots .
\end{align}
\subsection{Sub-leading contribution of relative entropy of coherent states}

We now apply the analytic continuations above to the averaged mixture of coherent states,
\begin{align}
    S\lb
    \rho_\alpha
    \Big\|
    \frac{\rho_\alpha+\rho_\beta}{2}
    \rb,
    \qquad
    \delta=\alpha-\beta .
\end{align}
For the current contribution,
\begin{align}
    \Delta_L=1,
    \qquad
    2\Delta_{L'}=\delta^2.
\end{align}
Using the result obtained above, the contribution from $\hat L_p^{\rm sum}$ is
\begin{align}
    \left.
    \Bigg[
    \log 2-
    S\lb
    \rho_\alpha
    \Big\|
    \frac{\rho_\alpha+\rho_\beta}{2}
    \rb
    \Bigg]
    \right|_{\hat L^{\rm sum}}
    &=
    -\frac{
    3\delta^2\,2^{\delta^2-3}
    B\lb
    1+\frac{\delta^2}{2},
    1+\frac{\delta^2}{2}
    \rb
    }{
    \pi\lb1+\frac{\delta^2}{2}\rb
    }
    \nn\\
    &\quad\times
    \Bigg[
    \delta^2+1
    +
    \frac{2(\delta^2-1)}{\pi^2}
    \psi^{(1)}
    \lb1+\frac{\delta^2}{2}\rb
    \Bigg]
    (\pi\ep)^{\delta^2+1}
    +\cdots .
\end{align}
Similarly, the contribution from $\hat L_p^{\rm div}$ is
\begin{align}
    \left.
    \Bigg[
    \log 2-
    S\lb
    \rho_\alpha
    \Big\|
    \frac{\rho_\alpha+\rho_\beta}{2}
    \rb
    \Bigg]
    \right|_{\hat L^{\rm div}}
    &=
    -\frac{
    3\delta^2\,2^{\delta^2-3}
    B\lb
    1+\frac{\delta^2}{2},
    1+\frac{\delta^2}{2}
    \rb
    }{
    \pi\lb1+\frac{\delta^2}{2}\rb
    }
    \nn\\
    &\quad\times
    \Bigg[
    1
    -
    \frac{2(\delta^2-1)}{\pi^2}
    \psi^{(1)}
    \lb1+\frac{\delta^2}{2}\rb
    \Bigg]
    (\pi\ep)^{\delta^2+1}
    +\cdots .
\end{align}
Adding the two contributions, the terms proportional to the trigamma function cancel, and we obtain
\begin{align}
    \left.
    \Bigg[
    \log 2-
    S\lb
    \rho_\alpha
    \Big\|
    \frac{\rho_\alpha+\rho_\beta}{2}
    \rb
    \Bigg]
    \right|_{\rm sub.}
    &=
    -\frac{
    3\delta^2\,2^{\delta^2-2}
    }{\pi}
    B\lb
    1+\frac{\delta^2}{2},
    1+\frac{\delta^2}{2}
    \rb
    (\pi\ep)^{\delta^2+1}
    +\cdots
    \nn\\
    &=
    -\frac{3\delta^2}{2\pi}
    \frac{
    \sqrt{\pi}\,
    \Gamma\lb1+\frac{\delta^2}{2}\rb
    }{
    4\Gamma\lb\frac32+\frac{\delta^2}{2}\rb
    }
    (\pi\ep)^{\delta^2+1}
    +\cdots .
\end{align}
Including the leading two-point contribution, we finally obtain
\begin{align}
    S\lb
    \rho_\alpha
    \Big\|
    \frac{\rho_\alpha+\rho_\beta}{2}
    \rb
    &=
    \log 2
    -
    \frac{
    \sqrt{\pi}\,
    \Gamma\lb1+\frac{\delta^2}{2}\rb
    }{
    4\Gamma\lb\frac32+\frac{\delta^2}{2}\rb
    }
    (\pi\ep)^{\delta^2}+
    \frac{3\delta^2}{2\pi}
    \frac{
    \sqrt{\pi}\,
    \Gamma\lb1+\frac{\delta^2}{2}\rb
    }{
    4\Gamma\lb\frac32+\frac{\delta^2}{2}\rb
    }
    (\pi\ep)^{\delta^2+1}
    +\cdots .
\end{align}
Thus, up to the first sub-leading order, the result depends only on
$\delta^2=(\alpha-\beta)^2$ and is therefore symmetric under
$\alpha\leftrightarrow\beta$. For $\delta^2>1$, the broken-word contribution starts at
$O(\ep^{2\delta^2})$ and does not affect the first sub-leading term. For
$\delta^2=1$, it contributes at the same order as the terms above and must be included separately.
\paragraph{Symmetry of coherent states relative entropy:}
For a generic CFT, the three-point function contribution derived in the previous appendix need not be symmetric under the exchange $\Psi \leftrightarrow\Phi$, since it consist of independent structures,
\begin{align}
    C_\Phi^L G_p(n) + C_\Psi^LG_{n-p}(n)
\end{align}
whose analytic continuation are in general different. However, for coherent states, the current algebra has an additional simplification. Writing, 
\begin{align}
    \delta = \alpha - \beta, \qquad s = \alpha + \beta,
\end{align}
the Ward identity gives, 
\begin{align}
    \frac{\braket{V_\delta V_{-\delta}J}}{\braket{V_\delta V_{-\delta}}}\propto \delta,
\end{align}
while the ordinary $\alpha \alpha$ and $\beta\beta$ OPEs carry current coefficients proportional to $\alpha$ and $\beta$ respectively. In addition, the two mixing OPEs contain current descendants proportional to $s$. Combining all current insertions, we find,
\begin{align}
    \hat L_p &= 
    2\delta^2\frac{\pi\ep}{n}
    \sum_{r=1}^{p-1}\cot\lb\frac{\pi r}{n}\rb
    +
    \delta^2\frac{\pi\ep}{n}
    \cot\lb\frac{\pi p}{n}\rb
    \nn\\
    &=
    \frac{\delta^2}{2}
    \frac{\pi\ep}{n}
    \sin\lb\frac{\pi p}{n}\rb
    \Big[
    G_p(n)+G_{n-p}(n)
    \Big] \\
    &\propto \delta^2
\end{align}
Thus, the two replica structures appear with the same effective coefficient and the dependence 
\section{Descendants}\label{Descendant contribution to Relative entropy}
\subsection{Level 2 descendant}
The two-point function can be first taylor expanded, to obtain,
\begin{align}
    S(\rho_\Psi \|\rho_\Phi)\Big|_{\braket{\mO_L\mO_L}} &= \sum_L\lb C_\Psi^L - C_\Phi^L\rb^2f_{LL}\sin^{2\Delta_L}(\pi x) \\
    &= \sum_L\lb C_\Psi^L - C_\Phi^L\rb^2f_{LL}(\pi x)^{2\Delta_L} \Bigg[1 - \frac{\Delta_L}{3}(\pi x)^2 + \cdots \Bigg].
\end{align}
Next, we consider the standard endpoint OPE \cite{yellowbook},
\begin{align}
    \Psi(z,\bar z)  \Psi ^*(0,0) = C_{\Psi}^L z^{h_L - 2h_\Psi}\bar z ^{\bar h_L - 2\bar h_{\Psi}} &\Bigg[ \mO_L + \beta_1 zL_{-1} \mO_L + \bar \beta_1 \bar z \bar L_{-1}\mO_L + z^2 \lb \beta_{11}L_{-1}^2+ \beta_2L_{-2}\rb \mO_L  \nn \\&\qquad + \bar z ^2 \lb \bar \beta_{11}\bar L_{-1}^2 + \bar\beta_2\bar L_{-2}\rb\mO_L + z\bar z \beta_1 \bar \beta_1 L_{-1} \bar L_{-1}\mO_L + \cdots \Bigg]
\end{align}
For our case, where we have non-mixing OPE, we have,
\begin{align}
    &\beta_1 = \bar \beta_1 = \frac{1}{2},\\
    &2(2h_L + 1)\beta_{11} + 3\beta_2 = \frac{h_L+1}{2},\\
    &6h_L \beta_{11}+ \lb 4h_L + \frac{c}{2}\rb \beta_2 = h_L+ h_\Psi 
\end{align}
Now, we translate the operators to the midpoint, to obtain the midpoint OPEs.
Note that, again the level-one term disappears at the midpoint. This can be seen, when we expand the first primary $\mO_L$ and the level-one descendant,
\begin{align}
    \mO_L\lb - l/2 \rb &\simeq \mO_L - \frac{l}{2}L_{-1}\mO_L + \cdots ,\\
    L_{-1}\mO_L\lb-\frac{l}{2}\rb &\simeq L_{-1}\mO_L + \cdots
\end{align}
where $\cdots$ refer to anti-holomorphic and level-two descendants. Hence, the coefficient of $L_{-1}\mO_L$, and the anti-holomorphic part is given by,
\begin{align}
    -\frac{1}{2} + \beta_1 = 0
\end{align}
which agrees with the result of the next subsection, that the odd level descendants vanish in midpoint OPEs.
We can now translate the level-two descendants,
\begin{align}
    \Bigg[ \lb\beta_{11} - \frac{1}{8}\rb L_{-1}^2 + \beta_2L_{-2} \Bigg]
\end{align}
where the $-\frac{1}{8}$ comes from translating the primary. 

For the $L_{-1}\bar L_{-1}$ descendant, we have contribution from translations of primary as well as level-one descendants. They all add up to zero. Hence we are left with the following midpoint OPE at the second-level order,
\begin{align}
    \Psi(l/2)\Psi^*(-l/2) &\simeq C_\Psi^L \Bigg[ \mO_L + l^2 \Bigg( \lb \beta_{11}-\frac{1}{8}\rb L_{-1}^2 + \beta_2L_{-2}\Bigg)\mO_L + \bar l^2 \Bigg( \lb \bar \beta_{11}-\frac{1}{8}\rb \bar L_{-1}^2 + \bar \beta_2\bar L_{-2}\Bigg)\mO_L + \cdots\Bigg] 
\end{align}
Now we can compute the contribution to the relative entropy from the level-two descendant terms, such as $\braket{\mO_L L_{-1}^2\mO_L}$ and $\braket{\mO_L L_{-2}\mO_L}$. The first one is easy to compute,
\begin{align}
    \braket{\mO_L(z,\bar z) L_{-1}^2\mO_L(0,0)} &= \p^2 \braket{\mO_L\mO_L} 
    \\
    &=\frac{2h_L (2h_L+1)}{z^{2h_L+2} \bar z^{2\bar h_L }}
\end{align}
The other level-two descendant can be computed as follows,
\begin{align}
    L_{-2}\mO_L (0) \oint_0 \frac{dw}{2\pi i } \frac{T(w)\mO_l(0)}{w}.
\end{align}
Using the conformal Ward identity,
\begin{align}
    T(w)\mO_L(z,\bar z)\mO_L(0,0) &= \Bigg[ \frac{h_L}{(w-z)^2}+ \frac{1}{(w-z)}\p_z + \frac{h_L}{w^2} + \frac{1}{w}\p_0 \Bigg]\braket{\mO_L\mO_L}
\end{align}
Now we multiply $1/w$ in the above equation, and take the residue at $w=0$. The non-vanishing contributions come from the operators at $z$. Therefore, we obtain,
\begin{align}
    \braket{\mO_L (z,\bar z) L_{-2}\mO_L} &= \frac{3h_L}{z^{2h_L + 2}\bar z ^{2\bar h_L}}
\end{align}
Now adding the contributions from the level-two descendants, with their OPE coefficients, we obtain,
\begin{align}
    2 \Bigg[ \lb \beta_{11}-\frac{1}{8} \rb\braket{\mO_L L_{-1}^2\mO_L} + \beta_2 \braket{\mO_L L_{-2}\mO_L}\Bigg] = \frac{\frac{h_L}{2}}{z^{2h_L+2}\bar z^{2\bar h_L}}.
\end{align}
The factor of 2 is due to the two different orientation, i.e. $\braket{\mO_L(z)L_{-1}^2\mO)L(w)}$ and $\braket{\mO_L(w)L_{-1}^2\mO)L(z)}$. Thus, the contribution to the relative entropy from the descendants is given by,
\begin{align}
    S(\rho_\Psi \|\rho_\Phi) \Big|_{[\mO_L\mO_L]_{\text{level-2}}} &= \sum_L \frac{\Delta_L}{2}(C_\Psi^L - C_\Phi^L)^2 \frac{\sqrt \pi \Gamma(\Delta_L + 2)}{4\Gamma(\Delta_L + 5/2)}(\pi x)^{2\Delta_L + 2} + \cdots\\
    &= \sum_L \frac{\Delta_L (\Delta_L + 1)}{2\Delta_L + 3} f_{\Delta_L,\Delta_L}(C_\Psi^L - C_\Phi^L)^2(\pi x)^{2\Delta_L + 2} + \cdots
\end{align}
Adding the subleading contribution from the taylor expansion of the two-point function, we finally obtain the contribution of the terms of the order $x^{2\Delta_L+ 2}$, 
\begin{align}
    S(\rho_\Psi \|\rho_\Phi)\Big|_{[\mO_L\mO_L]_{\text{level-2}}}&= \frac{\Delta_L^2}{3(2\Delta_L +3)}f_{\Delta_L,\Delta_L} (C_\Psi^L - C_\Phi^L)^2(\pi x)^{2\Delta_L + 2} + \cdots
\end{align}
\subsection{Odd level descendants}
In this subsection, using the method used in \cite{yellowbook}, we show that the odd level descendants do not contribute to the relative entropy. This is an artefact of using the midpoint OPEs. The idea would be to compare the OPE coefficients $\beta$ ($\tilde\beta$ in our case, which are the coefficients obtained after shifting operators to the midpoint) with the coefficients determined using conformal symmetry. Consider the three-point function,
\begin{align}
    \braket{\Psi(l/2) \Psi^*(-l/2) \mO_L(w)} &= C_{\Psi}^Ll^{h_L-2h_\Psi} \lb w^2 - \frac{l^2}{4}\rb^{-h_L}\\
    &\simeq C_{\Psi}^L l^{h_L-2h_\Psi}w^{-2h_L}\Bigg[ 1+ \frac{h_L}{4}\frac{l^2}{w^2} + \frac{h_L (h_L+1)}{32}\frac{l^4}{w^4}  + \cdots \Bigg]\label{eq: 3 point function expansion}
\end{align}
Now compare this the midpoint OPE,
\begin{align}
    \Psi(l/2) \Psi^*(-l/2) &= C_{\Psi}^Ll^{h_L-2h_\Psi} \Big[ \mO_L(0) + + l\tilde\beta_1 L_{-1}\mO_L(0) + l^2 \lb \tilde\beta_2L_{-2}+ \tilde \beta_{11}L_{-1}^2\rb\mO_L(0) \nn \\ &\qquad\qquad\qquad+ l^3\lb \tilde \beta_{111}L_{-1}^3 + \tilde \beta_{21}L_{-2}L_{-1} +  \tilde \beta _{3}L_{-3} \rb\mO_L(0) +\cdots \Big]
\end{align}
Now, taking the expectation value of the above OPE with $\mO_L(w)$, we obtain,
\begin{align}
    \braket{\Psi(l/2) \Psi^*(-l/2) \mO_L(w)} &= C_{\Psi}^Ll^{h_L-2h_\Psi} \Big[ \braket{\mO_L(0)\mO_L(w)} + l\braket{\tilde \beta_1 L_{-1}\mO_L(0)\mO_L(w)} \nn \\
    &\qquad+ l^2 \braket{\lb \tilde\beta_2L_{-2}+ \tilde \beta_{11}L_{-1}^2\rb\mO_L(0)\mO_L(w)} \nn \\ &\qquad+ l^3\braket{\lb \tilde \beta_{111}L_{-1}^3 + \tilde \beta_{21}L_{-2}L_{-1} +  \tilde \beta _{3}L_{-3} \rb\mO_L(0)\mO_L(w)} + \cdots  \Big]
\end{align}
However, from the earlier three-point function computation in (\ref{eq: 3 point function expansion}), we only had even powers of $l$. This suggests that the net contribution of every odd level descendant must vanish. 

\section{Large Interval for mixture of coherent states (Irrelevant, since this is just a check using exact 2n point function)}
\subsection{Exact analytical calculation}
\textbf{Leading and sub-leading terms}
For chiral vertex operators, the exact 2n-point correlation function is given by,
\begin{align}
    \Big\langle \prod_{l=1}^{2n}V_{q_l}(z_l)\Big\rangle = \prod_{l<r} (z_l - z_r)^{q_lq_r}.
\end{align}
Then, for the replica insertions, we have a word, 
$(a_0, a_1, \cdots, a_{n-1})$ for which the 2n-point function is given by,
\begin{align}
    \Bigg| \Big\langle \prod_{j=0}^{n-1}V_{a_j}(z_j^+) V_{-a_j}(z_j^-) \Big\rangle \Bigg| &= \prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\Big[ 2\sin \lb\frac{\pi x}{n}\rb  \Big]^{-a_j^2} \prod_{0\leq j <k \leq n-1}\Bigg[\frac{\lb2\sin\frac{\pi (k-j)}{n}\rb^2}{\Big|2\sin \frac{\pi (k-j-x)}{n}\Big| \Big|2\sin \frac{\pi (k-j+x)}{n}\Big|}\Bigg]^{a_ja_k}
\end{align}
We can also write the above expression as,
\begin{align}
\Bigg|
\Bigg\langle
\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}
V_{a_j}(z_j^+)\,
V_{-a_j}(z_j^-)
\Bigg\rangle
\Bigg|
&=
\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}
\left[
2\sin\left(\frac{\pi x}{n}\right)
\right]^{-a_j^2}
% \nonumber\\
% &\quad\times
\prod_{0\leq j<k\leq n-1}
\left[
2\sin\left(\frac{\pi(k-j)}{n}\right)
\right]^{2a_ja_k}
\nonumber\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{\substack{0\leq j,k\leq n-1\\ j\neq k}}
\left|
2\sin\left(
\frac{\pi(k-j-1+\epsilon)}{n}
\right)
\right|^{-a_ja_k}.
\end{align}
% \subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Contiguous $p=2$ word}{Contiguous p=2 word}}

% % \end{align}
% The ratio of the two words are given by,
% \begin{align}
%     \frac{\mathcal{C}_b^{(m)}}{\mathcal{C}_c} &=\Big[2\sin \frac{\pi \ep}{n}\Big]^{(\alpha -\beta)^2}\frac{\mathcal{R}_b^{(m)}}{\mathcal{R}_c} \\
%     &\propto \Big[2\sin \frac{\pi \ep}{n}\Big]^{(\alpha -\beta)^2}
% %     \left[
% % \frac{
% % \left(2\sin\frac{\pi}{n}\right)^2
% % \left|2\sin\frac{\pi(m-1+\epsilon)}{n}\right|
% % \left|2\sin\frac{\pi(m+1-\epsilon)}{n}\right|

% % \left(2\sin\frac{\pi m}{n}\right)^2
% % \left|2\sin\frac{\pi(2-\epsilon)}{n}\right|
% % }
% % \right]^{(\beta-\alpha)^2}.
% \end{align}
\subsubsection{Ratio of the two words}
Define the following,
\begin{align}
    \mathcal{P}_r(x) \equiv \frac{\lb 2 \sin \frac{\pi r}{n}\rb^2}{\big|2\sin \frac{\pi(r-x)}{n}\big| \big|2\sin \frac{\pi(r+x)}{n}\big|}
\end{align}
% Note that, $\mathcal{P}_{n-r} = \mathcal{P}_r$.
Then the contiguous word can be written as,
\begin{align}
\left|\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm c}(n,x)\right|
&=
\left[
2\sin\left(\frac{\pi x}{n}\right)
\right]^{-\left[2\beta^2+(n-2)\alpha^2\right]}
\mathcal{P}_{1}(n,x)^{\beta^2}
% \nonumber\\
% &\quad\times
\prod_{k=2}^{n-1}
\mathcal{P}_{k}(n,x)^{\alpha\beta}
\prod_{k=2}^{n-1}
\mathcal{P}_{k-1}(n,x)^{\alpha\beta}
\nonumber\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{2\leq j<k\leq n-1}
\mathcal{P}_{k-j}(n,x)^{\alpha^2}.
\end{align}
Similarly, the broken word is given by,
\begin{align}
\left|\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm b}^{(m)}(n,x)\right|
&=
\left[
2\sin\left(\frac{\pi x}{n}\right)
\right]^{-\left[2\beta^2+(n-2)\alpha^2\right]}
\mathcal{P}_{m}(n,x)^{\beta^2}
% \nonumber\\
% &\quad\times
\prod_{\substack{k=1\\ k\neq m}}^{n-1}
\mathcal{P}_{k}(n,x)^{\alpha\beta}
\prod_{j=1}^{m-1}
\mathcal{P}_{m-j}(n,x)^{\alpha\beta}
\nn\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{k=m+1}^{n-1}
\mathcal{P}_{k-m}(n,x)^{\alpha\beta}
% \nn\\
% &\quad\times
\prod_{\substack{0\leq j<k\leq n-1\\
                  j,k\neq 0,m}}
\mathcal{P}_{k-j}(n,x)^{\alpha^2}.
\end{align}
The first term in both the correlators cancel exactly. However, rather than comparing the large product separately, we compare the individual pairs in the two words. All pairs not involving sites 1 or m are exactly the same in both correlators and cancel. For the remaining pairs,

\textbf{Pair (0,1)}
Ratio is given by
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mathcal{P}_1(x)^{\alpha\beta}}{\mathcal{P}_1(x)^{\beta^2}} = \mathcal{P}_1(x)^{\alpha\beta-\beta^2}
\end{align}
\textbf{Pair (0,m)}
Ratio is given by
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mathcal{P}_m(x)^{\beta^2}}{\mathcal{P}_m(x)^{\alpha\beta}} = \mathcal{P}_m(x)^{\beta^2-\alpha\beta}
\end{align}
\textbf{Pair (1,m)}
Ratio is given by
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mathcal{P}_{m-1}(x)^{\alpha\beta}}{\mathcal{P}_{m-1}(x)^{\alpha\beta}} = 1
\end{align}
\textbf{Pair involving 1 or m and the remainging $\mathbf{\alpha}$ sites}
Let 
\begin{align}
    S = \{2,\cdots,n-1\} \backslash \{m\}
\end{align}
The remaining ratio is,
\begin{align}
    \prod_{k\in S} \lb\frac{\mathcal{P}_{|m-k|}(x)}{\mathcal{P}_{|1-k|}(x)}\rb^{\alpha\beta - \alpha^2}
\end{align}
Therefore we have,
\begin{align}
    \frac{\mathcal{C}_b^{(m)}(n,x)
    }{\mathcal{C}_c(n,x)}&= \mathcal{P}_1(x)^{\alpha\beta-\beta^2} \mathcal{P}_m(x)^{\beta^2-\alpha\beta}\prod_{k = 2, k\neq m}^{n-1} \lb\frac{\mathcal{P}_{|m-k|}(x)}{\mathcal{P}_{|1-k|}(x)}\rb^{\alpha\beta - \alpha^2}
\end{align}
We compute the numerator and denominator of the product in the right hand side separately. 
The numerator is given by,
\begin{align}
    \prod_{k = 2, k\neq m}^{n-1} \mathcal{P}_{|m-k|}(x) = \lb  \prod_{r=1}^{m-2}\mathcal{P}_r \rb\lb\prod_{r=1}^{n-1-m}\mathcal{P}_r\rb
\end{align}
Similarly the denominator is given by,
\begin{align}
    \prod_{k = 2, k\neq m}^{n-1}\mathcal{P}_{|1-k|} &=  \frac{\prod_{r=1}^{n-2}\mathcal{P}_r}{\mathcal{P}_{m-1}}\\
    &= \frac{\mathcal{P}_{m-1} \lb\prod_{r=1}^{m-2}\mathcal{P}_r\rb\lb\prod_{r=m}^{n-2}\mathcal{P}_r\rb}{\mathcal{P}_{m-1}} \\
    &= \lb\prod_{r=1}^{m-2}\mathcal{P}_r\rb\lb\prod_{r=m}^{n-2}\mathcal{P}_r\rb \\
    &= \lb\prod_{r=1}^{m-2}\mathcal{P}_r\rb\lb\prod_{s=2}^{n-m}\mathcal{P}_s\rb
\end{align}
where in the last step we used the symmetry $\mathcal{P}_{n-r} = \mathcal{P}_r$.
Consequently,
\begin{align}
    \prod_{k = 2, k\neq m}^{n-1} \lb\frac{\mathcal{P}_{|m-k|}(x)}{\mathcal{P}_{|1-k|}(x)}\rb &= \frac{\mathcal{P}_1(x)}{\mathcal{P}_m(x)}
\end{align}
Therefore,
\begin{align}
    \Bigg|\frac{\mathcal{C}_b^{(m)}(n,x)
    }{\mathcal{C}_c(n,x)}\Bigg| &= \mathcal{P}_1(x)^{\alpha\beta-\beta^2} \mathcal{P}_m(x)^{\beta^2-\alpha\beta} \lb \frac{\mathcal{P}_1(x)}{\mathcal{P}_m(x)} \rb^{\alpha\beta - \alpha^2}\\
    &= \lb \frac{\mathcal{P}_m(x)}{\mathcal{P}_1(x)} \rb^{(\alpha-\beta)^2} \\
    &= \lb\frac{\lb2\sin \frac{\pi m}{n}\rb^2 \big|2\sin \frac{\pi \ep}{n}\big| \big|2\sin \frac{\pi (2-\ep)}{n}\big|}{\lb2\sin \frac{\pi }{n}\rb^2 \big|2\sin \frac{\pi (m-1+\ep)}{n}\big|\big|2\sin \frac{\pi (m+1-\ep)}{n}\big|}\rb^{(\alpha-\beta)^2} \\
    &\underset{m\neq \{1,n-1\}}{\simeq}\lb2\sin \frac{\pi \ep}{n}\rb^{(\alpha-\beta)^2}\lb\frac{\lb2\sin \frac{\pi m}{n}\rb^2  \lb 2\sin \frac{2\pi }{n}\rb}{\lb2\sin \frac{\pi }{n}\rb^2 \lb2\sin \frac{\pi (m-1)}{n}\rb\lb2\sin \frac{\pi (m+1)}{n}\rb}\rb^{(\alpha-\beta)^2}, 
    % \quad m\neq \{1,n-1\}.
\end{align}



\subsection{Using OPEs}
\textbf{Leading and sub-leading terms}
From the denominator of (\ref{eq: mu (n,x,DeltaL')}), we assumed that the leading order contribution comes from the terms which have a string of $\Phi \Phi^*$ and a string of $\Psi \Psi^*$. That gave us mixing OPEs at two sites, while non-mixing OPEs at the remaining $n-2$ sites. For simplicity, consider the averaged mixture, i.e. $y=1/2$ case. For $p=2$ case, which corresponds to the insertions of two $\Phi \Phi^*$ and  $(n-2)$ $\Psi \Psi^*$ operators, we will compare two terms in the denominator. One would correspond to a single string of $\Phi \Phi^*$ connected to a string of $\Psi \Psi^*$. It will have 2 mixing OPE and $n-2$ non-mixing OPEs that include $n-3$ $\Psi \Psi^*$ and one $\Phi \Phi^*$ OPE. The other term would correspond to the case where the $\Phi \Phi^*$ insertions are separated, and hence there are 4 mixing OPEs, while $n-4$ $\Psi \Psi^*$ non-mixing OPEs. Particularly, we focus on chiral Vertex operators. So up to the leading order, we have the following OPEs,
\begin{align}
    V_\alpha V_{-\alpha} &= \zeta_n^{-\alpha^2} \lb  1 + \alpha \zeta_n J + \cdots\rb \\
    V_\beta V_{-\beta} &= \zeta_n^{-\beta^2} \lb  1 + \beta \zeta_n J + \cdots\rb \\
    V_\alpha V_{-\beta} &= \zeta_n^{-\alpha\beta} \lb  V_{\alpha - \beta} + \cdots\rb \\
    V_\beta V_{-\alpha} &= \zeta_n^{-\alpha\beta} \lb  V_{\beta - \alpha} + \cdots\rb
\end{align}

\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Contiguous $p=2$ word}{Contiguous p=2 word}}

We insert $V_\beta V_{-\beta}$ at $z_0$ and $z_1$. Thus, we have the following OPEs,
\begin{align}
    V_\beta(z_{1,n}^+) V_{-\alpha}(z_{2,n}^-) &= \zeta_n^{-\alpha \beta} \lb V_{\beta-\alpha} (e^{i\pi \theta_{1}})+ O(\zeta_n)\rb \\
    V_\alpha(z_{n-1,n}^+) V_{-\beta}(z_{0,n}^-) &= \zeta_n^{-\alpha \beta} \lb V_{\alpha-\beta}(e^{i\pi \theta_{n-1}}) + O(\zeta_n)\rb \\
    V_\alpha(z_{j,n}^+) V_{-\alpha}(z_{j,n}^-) &= \zeta_n^{-\alpha ^2} \lb 1 + O(\zeta_n)\rb, \qquad \qquad j = 2, \cdots, n-2.
\end{align}
The above $2n$ point function is thus given by,
\begin{align}
    &\zeta_n^{-(n-3)\alpha^2 -\beta^2 -2\alpha\beta} \braket{V_{\beta-\alpha}(e^{i \pi \theta_1})V_{\alpha - \beta}(e^{i \pi \theta_{n-1}})}\lb 1 + O(\zeta_n)\rb \\
    =& \zeta_n^{-(n-3)\alpha^2 -\beta^2 -2\alpha\beta} \lb2\sin(2\pi/n)\rb^{-(\alpha - \beta)^2} \lb 1+ O(\zeta_n)\rb
\end{align} 
% Normalizing the above correlator gives us,
% \begin{align}
%     &\frac{1}{\zeta_n^{-(n-2)\alpha^2 - 2\beta^2}}\zeta_n^{-(n-3)\alpha^2 -\beta^2 -2\alpha\beta} \lb2\sin(2\pi/n)\rb^{-(\alpha - \beta)^2} \lb 1+ \mO(\zeta_n)\rb \\
%     =&  \zeta_n^{(\alpha - \beta)^2} \lb2\sin(2\pi/n)\rb^{-(\alpha - \beta)^2} \lb 1+ \mO(\zeta_n)\rb
% \end{align}
\subsubsection{\texorpdfstring{Broken $p=2$ word}{Broken p=2 word}}
Now for the broken word, we insert $V_\beta V_{-\beta}$ at $z_0$ and $z_m$, where $1<m<n-1$. Thus, we have the following OPEs,
\begin{align}
    V_\beta(z_{0,n}^+) V_{-\alpha}(z_{1,n}^-) &= \zeta_n^{-\alpha \beta}\lb V_{\beta - \alpha}(e^{i\pi\theta_0}) + O(\zeta_n))\rb \\
    V_{\alpha}(z_{m-1,n}^+) V_{-\beta}(z_{m,n}^+) &= \zeta_n^{-\alpha\beta} \lb V_{\alpha -\beta}(e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}}) + O(\zeta_n) \rb \\
    V_\beta(z_{m,n}^+)V_{-\alpha}(z_{m+1n,}^-) &= \zeta_n^{-\alpha\beta} \lb V_{\beta - \alpha}(e^{i\pi \theta_{m}})+ O(\zeta_n)\rb \\
    V_\alpha(z_{n-1,n}^+)V_{-\beta}(z_{0,n}^-) &= \zeta_n^{-\alpha\beta} \lb V_{\alpha - \beta}(e^{i\pi \theta_{n-1}})+ O(\zeta_n)\rb 
\end{align}
Hence, we have the following 2n point function,
\begin{align}
    &\zeta_n^{-(n-4)\alpha^2 -4\alpha\beta} \braket{V_{\alpha - \beta}(e^{i\pi \theta_{n-1}})V_{\beta - \alpha}(e^{i\pi\theta_0})V_{\alpha -\beta}(e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}})V_{\beta - \alpha}(e^{i\pi \theta_{m}})}\lb 1+ O(\zeta_n)\rb \\
    =& \zeta_n^{-(n-4)\alpha^2 -4\alpha\beta} \lb\frac{(e^{i\pi\theta_{n-1}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}})(e^{i\pi\theta_{0}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m}})}{(e^{i\pi\theta_{n-1}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{0}})(e^{i\pi\theta_{n-1}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m}})(e^{i\pi\theta_{0}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}})(e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m}})}\rb^{(\alpha - \beta)^2} \lb 1 + O(\zeta_n)\rb \\
    =&\zeta_n^{-(n-4)\alpha^2 -4\alpha\beta}\lb\frac{(2\sin(\pi m /n))^2}{(2\sin(\pi/n))^2(2\sin(\pi(m-1)/n))(2\sin(\pi (m+1)/n))}\rb^{(\alpha - \beta)^2}  \lb 1 + O(\zeta_n)\rb 
\end{align}
Now we can compare the contiguous and broken correlators,
\begin{align}
    &\frac{\zeta_n^{-(n-4)\alpha^2 -4\alpha\beta} \braket{V_{\alpha - \beta}(e^{i\pi \theta_{n-1}})V_{\beta - \alpha}(e^{i\pi\theta_0})V_{\alpha -\beta}(e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}})V_{\beta - \alpha}(e^{i\pi \theta_{m}})}\lb 1+ O(\zeta_n)\rb}{\zeta_n^{-(n-3)\alpha^2 - \beta^2-2\alpha\beta}\lb2\sin(2\pi/n)\rb^{-(\alpha - \beta)^2} \lb 1+ O(\zeta_n)\rb}  \\
    % =& \frac{\zeta_n^{(\alpha-\beta)^2} \braket{V_{\alpha - \beta}(e^{i\pi \theta_{n-1}})V_{\beta - \alpha}(e^{i\pi\theta_0})V_{\alpha -\beta}(e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}})V_{\beta - \alpha}(e^{i\pi \theta_{m}})}\lb 1+ \mO(\zeta_n)\rb}{(2\sin(2\pi/n))^{-(\alpha-\beta)^2}} \\
    % =& \zeta_n^{(\alpha-\beta)^2} (2\sin(2\pi/n))^{(\alpha-\beta)^2}\lb\frac{(e^{i\pi\theta_{n-1}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}})(e^{i\pi\theta_{0}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m}})}{(e^{i\pi\theta_{n-1}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{0}})(e^{i\pi\theta_{n-1}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m}})(e^{i\pi\theta_{0}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}})(e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m}})}\rb^{(\alpha - \beta)^2} \nn \\ 
    % & \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \times  \lb 1+ \mO(\zeta_n)\rb \\
    =& \zeta_n^{(\alpha-\beta)^2} \lb\frac{(2\sin(2\pi/n))(2\sin(\pi m /n))^2}{(2\sin(\pi/n))^2(2\sin(\pi(m-1)/n))(2\sin(\pi (m+1)/n))}\rb^{(\alpha - \beta)^2}\lb 1+ O(\zeta_n)\rb 
\end{align}
% Normalizing this gives us the following,
% \begin{align}
%     &\frac{1}{\zeta_n^{-(n-2)\alpha^2 - 2\beta^2}} \zeta_n^{-(n-4)\alpha^2 -4\alpha\beta} \braket{V_{\alpha - \beta}(e^{i\pi \theta_{n-1}})V_{\beta - \alpha}(e^{i\pi\theta_0})V_{\alpha -\beta}(e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}})V_{\beta - \alpha}(e^{i\pi \theta_{m}})}\lb 1+ \mO(\zeta_n)\rb \\
%     =& \zeta_n^{-2(\alpha-\beta)^2} \braket{V_{\alpha - \beta}(e^{i\pi \theta_{n-1}})V_{\beta - \alpha}(e^{i\pi\theta_0})V_{\alpha -\beta}(e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}})V_{\beta - \alpha}(e^{i\pi \theta_{m}})}\lb 1+ \mO(\zeta_n)\rb \\
%     =& \zeta_n^{-2(\alpha-\beta)^2} \lb\frac{(e^{i\pi\theta_{n-1}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}})(e^{i\pi\theta_{0}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m}})}{(e^{i\pi\theta_{n-1}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{0}})(e^{i\pi\theta_{n-1}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m}})(e^{i\pi\theta_{0}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}})(e^{i\pi\theta_{m-1}} - e^{i\pi\theta_{m}})}\rb^{(\alpha - \beta)^2} \lb 1+ \mO(\zeta_n)\rb \\
%     =& \zeta_n^{-2(\alpha-\beta)^2} \lb\frac{(2\sin(\pi m /n))(2\sin (\pi m/n))}{(2\sin(\pi/n))^2(2\sin(\pi(m-1)/n))(2\sin(\pi (m+1)/n))}\rb^{(\alpha - \beta)^2}\lb 1+ \mO(\zeta_n)\rb
% \end{align}
Since $\zeta_n = 2\sin(\pi \ep/n) \sim 2\pi \ep/n$, the ratio tends to zero.  
Similarly, for  $p>1$, it can be shown that the term having the least number of mixing OPEs would be the leading term. Hence, in our calculations the dominant contribution came from the terms which had a contiguous string of $\Psi \Psi^*$ connected to a contiguous string of $\Phi \Phi^*$. These calculations can be extended for any general 2d CFT, since we just require the order of the terms.

\section{Numerical methods}
\label{app:num-methods}
This appendix specifies the numerical calculations used in
Sec.~\ref{sec:num-examples}. The calculations evaluate finite-chain
reduced density matrices directly.

\subsection{States, subsystems, and sampling}
\label{app:num-sampling}
We use periodic spin-1/2 chains with $N$ sites and unit lattice spacing, so their
circumference is $R=N$ and the interval length $l$ counts sites. We take contiguous
subsystems $A=\{0,\ldots,l-1\}$, $B=A^c$. All active mixture
fits and figures use $l=6,7,8,9,10$. The Ising states are
$0$, $\sigma$, and $\varepsilon$; the boson states are
$00$, $ss$, and $3s,s$. Each of the three
unordered pairs is evaluated in both directions. We use natural
logarithms and the incoherent mixture
$\bar\rho_C=(\rho_C^\Psi+\rho_C^\Phi)/2$, for $C=A,B$.
For both equal and unequal weights, the complete system-size grid is
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal G_m(c)=\{(N,l):N\in4\mathbb N,\ 6\leq l\leq10,
 \ l\in\mathbb N,\ N\leq N_{\max}^{(m)},\ 0<l/N\leq c\},
 \label{eq:num-extended-grid}
\end{equation}
where $N_{\max}^{({\rm Ising})}=8192$ and
$N_{\max}^{({\rm boson})}=16384$. The evaluated grids use
$c=0.1$ for small intervals and $c=0.05$ for large complements.
At each fixed source weight they contain $183\,168$ and
$181\,980$ directed observations, respectively, or $365\,148$
in total. All required geometry,
state-pair, and direction records are present, with no duplicate
records. Chain sizes are uniformly spaced by four; the fractions
$l/N$ are not uniformly spaced. All displayed mixture fits use
$c=0.02$ and $c=0.005$, respectively. For
Sec.~\ref{sec:num-general-y}, replace $\bar\rho_C$ by
$\rho_{C,y}=y\rho_C^\Psi+(1-y)\rho_C^\Phi$ and repeat the complete
grid at $y_j=(78+j)/195$, $j=0,\ldots,39$. These forty probabilities
yield $7\,326\,720$ small-interval and $7\,279\,200$ large-interval
observations, in addition to the separately retained half-weight
reference. Each direction is indexed by its source weight; the
reverse direction to the same mixture has weight $1-y$.

For each group, observations are selected individually by their
fraction $l/R$: a chain need not contribute all five lengths
inside the fitting window. Repeated fractions arising from
different $(N,l)$ remain distinct observations.
Table.~\ref{tab:num-windows} gives the windows and counts.
The interval figures show only fitted observations; lengths share
markers and colors, while residual colors identify models. In the
probability figures, each marker is an independently fitted leading
coefficient, and the marker style identifies the source direction.
\begin{table}[htbp]\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}\toprule
Regime & CFT & Upper cutoff & Points & Sizes & Min. training\\\midrule
Small $x$ & Ising & $0.02$ & 9744 & 1974 & 9739\\
Small $x$ & Boson & $0.02$ & 19984 & 4022 & 19979\\
Small $\ep$ & Ising & $0.005$ & 8245 & 1749 & 8240\\
Small $\ep$ & Boson & $0.005$ & 18485 & 3797 & 18480\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Complete step-4 sampling per ordered pair and source weight
for all three fitting models. The point and size counts apply to
$y=1/2$ and to each of the forty unequal weights. The lower
endpoint is zero and is open. ``Min. training'' gives the smallest
number of observations left after omitting a complete chain size
in the equal-mixture sensitivity checks. These are deterministic lattice data, not independent
random samples.}
\label{tab:num-windows}
\end{table}

\subsection{Exact Ising primary states and interval matrices}
\label{app:num-ising}
We use the critical periodic spin Hamiltonian
\begin{equation}
 H=-\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}X_jX_{j+1}-\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}Z_j,
 \qquad X_N=X_0,\qquad P=\prod_j Z_j.
 \label{eq:num-ising-H}
\end{equation}
The Jordan--Wigner Majoranas
$\gamma_{2j}=(\prod_{k<j}Z_k)X_j$ and
$\gamma_{2j+1}=(\prod_{k<j}Z_k)Y_j$ reduce this Hamiltonian
to a quadratic form~\cite{numMontes2017,numPeschelEisler}.
Even spin parity gives antiperiodic fermions. Their vacuum
represents $0$, and occupying the two modes at momenta
$\pm\pi/N$ represents $\varepsilon$. Odd spin parity gives
periodic fermions; their lowest state with the zero-mode
occupation fixed to $P=-1$ represents $\sigma$. All three
states have zero spin momentum. Their exact energies are
\begin{equation}
 E_0=-2\csc\frac{\pi}{2N},\qquad
 E_\sigma=-2\cot\frac{\pi}{2N},\qquad
 E_\varepsilon=E_0+8\sin\frac{\pi}{2N}.
 \label{eq:num-ising-energies}
\end{equation}
With velocity two, these gaps approach the scaling dimensions
$0,1/8,1$, respectively.

For reproducibility, the real antisymmetric covariance is
$G_{rs}=\langle i\gamma_r\gamma_s\rangle$ for $r\ne s$ and
$G_{rr}=0$. Set $L_M=2N$, $k_m=2\pi(m+t)/L_M$, and
$w_m=-\operatorname{sgn}(\sin k_m)$. The twist is $t=1/2$
for $(0,\varepsilon)$ and $t=0$ for $\sigma$. Reverse $w_m$
at $m=0,N-1,N,L_M-1$ for $\varepsilon$. For $\sigma$, set
$w_0=w_N=0$ and include the parity-fixing term in
\begin{equation}
 (G_{\Psi})_{rs}=\frac{i}{L_M}\sum_{m=0}^{L_M-1}w_m e^{ik_m(r-s)}
 +\begin{cases}[(-1)^r-(-1)^s]/L_M,&\Psi=\sigma,\\0,&\Psi=0,\varepsilon.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:num-covariance}
\end{equation}
This exact Fourier construction avoids a full Hamiltonian
eigensolve. We check antisymmetry, purity $G_{\Psi}^2=-\mathbb{I}$,
and the energies in Eq.~\eqref{eq:num-ising-energies}.

Restrict $G_{\Psi}$ to the first $2l$ Majoranas. If an orthogonal
change of basis gives blocks
$\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&g_j\\-g_j&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$,
and $b_r$ are the correspondingly rotated Majoranas, then
\begin{equation}
 \rho_A^\Psi=2^{-l}\prod_{j=0}^{l-1}
       (\mathbb{I}+g_j i b_{2j}b_{2j+1}).
 \label{eq:num-ising-rdm}
\end{equation}
All Jordan--Wigner strings lie inside this interval, so this is
also its spin density matrix. We form the $2^l$-dimensional
component matrices and then their mixture. Although each
component is Gaussian, the mixture generally is not; its
entropy is evaluated from the actual matrix, not an averaged
covariance.

\subsection{Compact-boson states and exact environment contraction}
\label{app:num-boson}
The compactification radius of our compact free boson CFT example is $R_{\rm c}=\sqrt2$. In the vertex notation of
the main text, $(\alpha,\bar\alpha)=(n_L,n_R)/\sqrt2$ and
$(h,\bar h)=(\alpha^2,\bar\alpha^2)/2$.
The integer charge labels $(n_L,n_R)=(0,0),(1,1),(3,1)$ correspond
to $\ket{00},\ket{ss},\ket{3s,s}$, respectively. On sites
$z_j=e^{2\pi i j/N}$, the auxiliary vertex-block construction
with charges at zero and $W$ gives a Jastrow polynomial
\cite{numHerwerth}. For an occupied set $O$ its normalized
amplitude is
\begin{align}
 \psi_{n_L,n_R}(O)&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal Z_{n_L,n_R}}}
 \prod_{j\in O}z_j^{n_L+1}(1-z_j/W)^{n_R}
 \prod_{i<j\in O}(z_j-z_i)^2,\nonumber\\
 |O|&=\frac{N-n_L-n_R}{2},\qquad W=10^8.
 \label{eq:num-boson-wavefunction}
\end{align}
$\mathcal Z_{n_L,n_R}$ is the sum of squared amplitudes before
normalization. The three states have occupations $N/2,N/2-1,N/2-2$
and are exactly orthogonal. The complex phase of this amplitude is retained in every off-diagonal matrix element. The finite endpoint $W$ is part of the supplied lattice-state convention; it approximates the strict insertion at infinity.

To compute both density matrices and cross-state transitions,
let $U,V\subset A$ be occupied sets and let $Y\subset B$ be
their common environment. For states $\Psi_1=\Psi$, $\Psi_2=\Phi$, with occupations
$M_i$ and unnormalized polynomials $P_i$, we need
\begin{equation}
 (\mathsf T_A^{12})_{UV}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal Z_1\mathcal Z_2}}
 \sum_{\substack{Y\subset B\\|Y|=M_1-|U|=M_2-|V|}}
       P_1(U\cup Y)P_2(V\cup Y)^*.
 \label{eq:num-boson-transition}
\end{equation}
It vanishes if the two required environment occupations differ.

We contract this sum through confluent Vandermonde identities
and Pfaffians, building on the moment method for the
Haldane--Shastry state~\cite{numStephan}. Explicitly, set
$m=M_1-|U|=M_2-|V|$, $\nu_j=\mathbf1_{j\in U}+\mathbf1_{j\in V}$,
$d=M_1+M_2$, $g_i(z)=(1-z/W)^{n_{R,i}}$, and
\begin{equation}
    \begin{aligned}
       &L_i(U)=\prod_{j\in U}z_j^{n_{L,i}+1}g_i(z_j)
                  \prod_{j<k\in U}(z_j-z_k)^2,\nonumber\\
 &w(z)=z^{n_{L,1}-n_{L,2}-2M_2+2}g_1(z)\overline{g_2(z)},\nonumber\\
 &\mathcal E_{UV}=\sum_{\substack{Y\subset B\\|Y|=m}}
       \prod_{i<j\in Y}(z_i-z_j)^4
       \prod_{i\in Y}\left[w(z_i)\prod_{j\in A}(z_j-z_i)^{2\nu_j}\right],
    \end{aligned}
     \label{eq:num-environment} 
\end{equation}
so that $(\mathsf T_A^{12})_{UV}=L_1(U)\overline{L_2(V)}
\prod_{j\in V}z_j^{-2m}\mathcal E_{UV}/\sqrt{\mathcal Z_1\mathcal Z_2}$.
Let $f(z)=(1,z,\ldots,z^{d-1})^T$ and define
\begin{equation}
 \mathsf M_B=\sum_{j\in B}w(z_j)
 [f(z_j)f'(z_j)^T-f'(z_j)f(z_j)^T].
 \label{eq:num-moment}
\end{equation}
At each site of $A$, a matrix $E_{UV}$ contains no column if
$\nu_j=0$, $f(z_j)$ if $\nu_j=1$, and $f(z_j),f'(z_j)$ if
$\nu_j=2$, in site order. With $q=\sum_j\nu_j$ and
$\Delta_{\rm ext}=\prod_{i<j\in A}(z_j-z_i)^{\nu_i\nu_j}$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal E_{UV}=\frac{(-1)^{q(q-1)/2}}{\Delta_{\rm ext}}
   \operatorname{Pf}\begin{pmatrix}\mathsf M_B&E_{UV}\\-E_{UV}^T&0\end{pmatrix}.
 \label{eq:num-bordered-pf}
\end{equation}
Here $d+q$ is even, including for transitions with odd $d$.
The formula defines the contraction without requiring an
inverse of a possibly singular cross-moment matrix. Numerical evaluation uses sparse full-circle moments, a rank-$2l$
subtraction of the interval sites. This extends the norm contraction to off-diagonal reduced matrices and transitions.

% Cancellation in the moment algebra requires 100 decimal digits
% through $N=256$ and 120 digits at the added sizes. Sampled
% entries are independently checked at 160 digits using direct
% pivoted Pfaffians. Only the final reduced matrices are converted
% to double precision. 

\subsection{Large intervals from a small Gram matrix}
\label{app:num-gram}
A global pure state has Schmidt rank at most $2^l$ across
the cut, even though $B$ has $N-l$ sites. The mixture on $B$
therefore has rank at most $2^{l+1}$. Its calculation needs
the transition $\mathsf T_A^{\Psi\Phi}=\tr_B\ket\Psi\bra\Phi$, as well as
$\rho_A^\Psi,\rho_A^\Phi$; the individual reduced states alone do
not specify their Schmidt-support overlap on $B$.

Writing $\ket\Psi=\sum_{u,v}X_{\Psi}(v,u)\ket u_A\ket v_B$ and
$Z=(X_{\Psi},X_{\Phi})/\sqrt2$ gives $ZZ^\dagger=\bar\rho_B$ and
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal G=Z^\dagger Z=\frac12
 \begin{pmatrix}(\rho_A^\Psi)^T&(\mathsf T_A^{\Psi\Phi})^*\\
 (\mathsf T_A^{\Psi\Phi})^T&(\rho_A^\Phi)^T\end{pmatrix}.
 \label{eq:num-gram}
\end{equation}
The nonzero eigenvalues $\lambda_k$ of $\mathcal G$ equal
those of $\bar\rho_B$. For its normalized eigenvectors $v_k$
and the first-block projector $P_{\Psi}$, the directed entropy is
\begin{equation}
 S(\rho_B^\Psi\|\bar\rho_B)=\tr(\rho_A^\Psi\log\rho_A^\Psi)
 -2\sum_{\lambda_k>0}\lambda_k(v_k^\dagger P_{\Psi}v_k)\log\lambda_k.
 \label{eq:num-gram-entropy}
\end{equation}
Purity supplies the first term, and the second follows from
$X_{\Psi}=\sqrt2ZE_{\Psi}$, where $E_{\Psi}$ embeds the first block.
The reversed direction uses $P_{\Phi}$ and $\rho_A^\Phi$.
We diagonalize common parity sectors on $B$ for Ising and
common occupation sectors for the boson. A constant phase on $\mathsf T_A^{\Psi\Phi}$ is immaterial, but its
configuration-dependent phases must be retained.

For Ising, direct Gaussian transition formulas between the
orthogonal primaries would divide by zero overlap. We instead
choose a local Hermitian unitary $U_A=\gamma_0=X_0$ for pairs
containing $\sigma$, or $U_A=i\gamma_0\gamma_1=-Z_0$ for
$(0,\varepsilon)$, and set $\ket{\Phi'}=U_A\ket\Phi$.
This gives a nonzero anchor overlap on the sampled grid.
Gaussian product identities~\cite{numBravyi,numSurace} give
\begin{align}
 K&=\mathbb{I}-G_{\Psi}G_{\Phi'},&
 o&=2^{-N/2}|\det K|^{1/4},\nonumber\\
 KZ_A&=(\mathbb{I}-iG_{\Psi})E_A,&
 G_{*,A}&=i\left[E_A^T(\mathbb{I}-iG_{\Phi'})Z_A-\mathbb{I}_{2l}\right],
 \label{eq:num-anchor-solve}
\end{align}
where $E_A$ selects the first $2l$ coordinates. Fourier
application of $G_{\Psi}$ and a pivoted real LU factorization of
$K$ avoid forming a full complex inverse; its log determinant
also gives $\log o$. For a complex antisymmetric $H$ on the
interval modes, Wick reconstruction is
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R(H)=2^{-l}
 \sum_{\substack{S\subset\{0,\ldots,2l-1\}\\|S|\ \mathrm{even}}}
 i^{|S|/2}\operatorname{Pf}(H_S)
       \prod_{r\in S}^{\longrightarrow}\gamma_r.
 \label{eq:num-wick}
\end{equation}
The product is ordered by increasing index and the empty
Pfaffian is one. Restoring the anchor yields
$\mathsf T_A^{\Psi\Phi}=o\mathcal R(G_{*,A})U_A$, up to a single irrelevant
phase. Independent small-chain wavefunction checks validate
this use of the Gaussian identities. The boson transitions
are obtained directly from Eq.~\eqref{eq:num-boson-transition}.

\subsection{Entropy evaluation and control of cancellation}
\label{app:num-precision}
For ordinary fractions, diagonalizing the component and mixture
matrices gives $S(\rho\|\bar\rho)=\tr\rho\log\rho-\tr\rho\log\bar\rho$.
The cross trace uses the weights of $\rho$ in the eigenbasis
of $\bar\rho$; the matrices need not commute. The primary check
uses the same spectral trace with $\bar\rho$ replaced by $\rho_A^\Phi$
and a target-logarithm floor of $10^{-15}$.

At very small fractions, subtracting nearly equal entropy traces,
or subtracting $S(\rho_B^\Psi\|\bar\rho_B)$ from $\log2$, loses relative precision.
We evaluate the remainder directly using matrix-function
divided differences~\cite{numHigham}. Let $f(t)=t\log t$,
$D=\operatorname{diag}(d_i)>0$, and
$H=D+E=V\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_k)V^\dagger$. Then
\begin{align}
 \mathcal R_i&=[f(H)-f(D)]_{ii}-f'(d_i)E_{ii}\nonumber\\
 &=\sum_k|(V^\dagger E)_{ki}|^2
 \frac{\lambda_k\log(\lambda_k/d_i)-\lambda_k+d_i}
      {(\lambda_k-d_i)^2}.
 \label{eq:num-stable-remainder}
\end{align}
The identity follows by expanding in the eigenvectors of $H$
and using $(\lambda_k-d_i)V_{ik}=(EV)_{ik}$. At $\lambda=d$ the quotient is $1/(2d)$. With
$u=(\lambda-d)/d$, its stable form is
\begin{equation}
 \frac1d\frac{(1+u)\log(1+u)-u}{u^2}
 =\frac1d\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-u)^n}{(n+1)(n+2)}.
 \label{eq:num-scalar-remainder}
\end{equation}
Ten terms are used for $|u|<0.02$, where the omitted tail is
below double-precision accuracy; otherwise the closed expression
is used.

For a small interval, take $D$ to be the diagonalized mixture
and $H$ the source density matrix in that basis. Equal traces
give $S(\rho_A^\Psi\|\bar\rho_A)=\sum_i\mathcal R_i$. For the complement,
take the unweighted Gram matrix
\begin{equation}
 H=\begin{pmatrix}\rho_A^\Psi&\mathsf T_A^{\Psi\Phi}\\
 (\mathsf T_A^{\Psi\Phi})^\dagger&\rho_A^\Phi\end{pmatrix}=2\mathcal G^*.
 \label{eq:num-unweighted-gram}
\end{equation}
Diagonalize its two diagonal blocks separately and let $D$
contain their eigenvalues. The perturbation $E$ then has only
off-diagonal blocks. Equation~\eqref{eq:num-gram-entropy} gives
\begin{align}
 \log2-S(\rho_B^\Psi\|\bar\rho_B)
 &=\tr P_{\Psi}[f(H)-f(D)]
 =\sum_i(P_{\Psi})_{ii}\mathcal R_i,\nonumber\\
 \log2-S(\rho_B^\Phi\|\bar\rho_B)
 &=\sum_i(P_{\Phi})_{ii}\mathcal R_i.
 \label{eq:num-stable-deficit}
\end{align}
This permits a deficit much smaller than machine precision
relative to $\log2$ to be resolved on its own scale.

For unequal weights, use the weighted Gram matrix
\begin{equation}
 H_y=\begin{pmatrix}
 y\rho_A^\Psi&\sqrt{y(1-y)}\,\mathsf T_A^{\Psi\Phi}\\
 \sqrt{y(1-y)}\,(\mathsf T_A^{\Psi\Phi})^\dagger&(1-y)\rho_A^\Phi
 \end{pmatrix}.
 \label{eq:num-weighted-gram}
\end{equation}
After diagonalizing its two diagonal blocks, let $D_y$ contain
their eigenvalues and evaluate the same remainders
$\mathcal R_i(H_y,D_y)$ in Eq.~\eqref{eq:num-stable-remainder}.
The two deficits to this fixed mixture are
\begin{align}
 -\log y-S(\rho_B^\Psi\|\rho_{B,y})
 &=\frac1y\sum_{i\in\Psi}\mathcal R_i(H_y,D_y),\nonumber\\
 -\log(1-y)-S(\rho_B^\Phi\|\rho_{B,y})
 &=\frac1{1-y}\sum_{i\in\Phi}\mathcal R_i(H_y,D_y).
 \label{eq:num-weighted-deficit}
\end{align}
Thus the large-interval probability data are obtained directly as
stable deficits, without subtracting nearly equal entropies.
For small intervals the diagonalized target is simply
$\rho_{A,y}$, and the same trace remainder gives the relative entropy.

The identities are exact on positive support. In the
double-precision implementation, reference eigenvalues below
$\tau=10^{-15}$ are discarded within each symmetry sector.
This support truncation is a numerical approximation. The returned
equal-mixture production checkpoints record $816\,558$ passing
numerical checks. The unequal-weight run records another
$12\,142\,275$ passing checks across all $6116$ model--size jobs,
including density-matrix constraints, retained-support sensitivity,
Gram positivity, and reproduction of the saved half-weight values.
All returned checkpoints pass the completeness audit, and every
reported unequal-weight fit has been replayed from the merged data.
These checks concern the finite-chain calculations, not the
lattice-to-CFT discrepancy. In particular, agreement of two
trace-log evaluations in absolute error alone cannot establish
the relative accuracy of a very small deficit.
% $\tau=10^{-14},10^{-16}$ at sampled endpoint lengths, the ordinary spectral trace, and the full Gram calculation. Table.~\ref{tab:num-checks} records the supplied production diagnostics; its cutoff sensitivities refer to retained endpoint lengths $6,10$ for the dense scan and $10$ for the earlier extension. Absolute trace-log agreement alone cannot certify the relative accuracy of an extremely small deficit.

\subsection{Fitting models, residuals, and window dependence}
\label{app:num-fitting}
Use $z=x$ for a small interval and $z=\ep=1-x$ for a large
interval. Let $v_i$ denote the corresponding measured value of
$S(\rho_A^\Psi\|\bar\rho_A)$ or
$\log2-S(\rho_B^\Psi\|\bar\rho_B)$. At unequal weight these become
$S(\rho_A^\Psi\|\rho_{A,y})$ and
$-\log y-S(\rho_B^\Psi\|\rho_{B,y})$, respectively; each weight
and source direction is fitted independently on the same geometry grid.
For each ordered pair $(\Psi,\Phi)$, we compare
\begin{equation}
 M_{\rm free}(z)=C_{\rm free}z^r,\qquad
 M_1(z)=c_0z^{r_0},\qquad
 M_2(z)=c_0z^{r_0}+c_1z^{r_1}.
 \label{eq:num-fits}
\end{equation}
The free fit estimates $C_{\rm free}>0$ and $r$ without theoretical
constraints; its fitted exponent is denoted $\widehat r$.
$M_1$ and $M_2$ impose the powers in
Table.~\ref{tab:num-powers} and fit one and two amplitudes,
respectively; the $M_2$ amplitudes have no sign constraints.
In the equal-mixture interval figures, the CFT curve includes the analytical leading term and each
complete known next term: the small-Ising sixth-order terms and
the large-boson sixth- and third-order terms listed in
Table~\ref{tab:num-powers}. In the other channels it includes
the leading term only. The probability figures instead compare
the fitted leading coefficient directly with $c_0^{\rm CFT}(y)$.
For unequal weights, the present next-coefficient comparison is
restricted to the known small-Ising $0\leftrightarrow\varepsilon$
sixth-order terms.
\begin{table}[htbp]\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}\toprule
 & & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Small interval} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{Large interval}\\
CFT & Pair (both directions) & $r_0$ & $r_1$ & $r_0=2\Delta_{L'}$ & $r_1$\\\midrule
Ising & $(0,\sigma)$ & 2 & 4 & $1/4$ & $1/2$\\
Ising & $(0,\varepsilon)$ & 4 & 6 & 2 & 4\\
Ising & $(\sigma,\varepsilon)$ & 2 & 4 & $1/4$ & $1/2$\\
Boson & $(00,ss)$ & 2 & 4 & 1 & 2\\
Boson & $(00,(3s,s))$ & 2 & 4 & 5 & 6\\
Boson & $(ss,(3s,s))$ & 2 & 4 & 2 & 3\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Powers used in $M_1,M_2$. At $y=1/2$, complete next coefficients are
available for the small-interval Ising $0\leftrightarrow\varepsilon$
pair and the large-interval boson $00\leftrightarrow3s,s$ and
$ss\leftrightarrow3s,s$ pairs. The other next amplitudes remain
numerical estimates without a complete analytical comparison.
The unequal-weight next-coefficient tests use the small-Ising
$0\leftrightarrow\varepsilon$ pair.}
\label{tab:num-powers}
\end{table}

Every fit minimizes unweighted squared residuals in the
observable, $\sum_{i:z_i\in W}[v_i-M(z_i)]^2$, on the window
in Table.~\ref{tab:num-windows}. For fixed powers, scale the columns as
$X_{ij}=(z_i/z_\star)^{r_j}$, where $z_\star$ is the upper
cutoff, solve by singular-value decomposition, and convert
the fitted amplitudes back by dividing by $z_\star^{r_j}$.
For a trial free exponent the optimal scaled amplitude is
\begin{equation}
 A(r)=\frac{\sum_i v_i(z_i/z_\star)^r}{\sum_i(z_i/z_\star)^{2r}},\qquad C_{\rm free}=A(r)z_\star^{-r}.
 \label{eq:num-profile}
\end{equation}
A scalar search of the profiled objective provides an
independent check on the two-parameter optimization
\cite{numGolubPereyra}. The signed amplitude discrepancies are
\begin{equation}
 \eta_j(M)[\%]=100\left(\frac{c_j(M)}{c_j^{\rm CFT}}-1\right),
 \qquad j=0,1.
 \label{eq:num-residuals}
\end{equation}
Here $\eta_0$ is reported for both $M_1$ and $M_2$, and $\eta_1$
only for $M_2$ where a complete nonzero $c_1^{\rm CFT}$ is available.
All discrepancies are computed from the unrounded fitted amplitudes.
For large intervals, both fitted and analytical coefficients refer to
the deficit $\log2-S$ at $y=1/2$, and to $-\log y-S$ at
unequal source weight $y$.
The pointwise figure residual and the free-power discrepancy are
\begin{equation}
 R_i(M)[\%]=100\frac{v_i-M(z_i)}{v_i},\qquad
 \eta_r[\%]=100\left(\frac{\widehat r}{r_0}-1\right).
 \label{eq:num-point-residuals}
\end{equation}
Residual ordinates are linear. Omitting all observations at one
chain size and refitting tests the sensitivity of the coefficients;
it does not give a statistical confidence interval for these
deterministic calculations. In particular, a decrease in the
training residual is guaranteed by the nesting of $M_1$ in $M_2$
and is not itself evidence of more accurate CFT coefficients.

The common windows in Table~\ref{tab:num-windows} provide a
fixed comparison across both models and every direction. They
are not windows of uniformly one-percent amplitude accuracy.
For $y=1/2$, an exhaustive scan of the observed large-interval endpoints,
including zero-anchored intervals, gives $85\,059\,733$ selections
with at least forty observations and all five lengths for each
CFT. None simultaneously has $|\eta_0(M_2)|\leq1\%$ in every
direction and $|\eta_1(M_2)|\leq10\%$ wherever the next
coefficient is known, even before imposing $|\eta_r|\leq1\%$.
This is a result for the present finite grid and unweighted
fitting models, not a statement about the continuum limit.
Small $l/R$ and large $l$ are separate requirements; increasing
$N$ at fixed $l=6,\ldots,10$ does not remove finite-length effects.
